### OR
## THE HANDBOOK <note>original: "Vade Mecum," literally "Go with me"—a manual intended to be carried at all times.</note>
### OR, CONCERNING THE NUMBER
### OF PHILOSOPHERS,
### a clause of the Testament.

## RAYMOND LULL <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1316) was a Majorcan philosopher and polymath; many alchemical works were later attributed to him.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'D' featuring floral and leaf patterns.</image-desc>
God, by the power of the Trinity—through which the unity of your divinity is neither harmed nor confounded in any way—we begin this present Compendium. We command that it be named otherwise the *Handbook of the Number of Philosophers*, or the *Codicil Clause of the Testament*. This work is now granted by our perpetual will to our successive heirs, the renowned Kings of the English <note>Legend claims Lull performed alchemical transmutations for King Edward of England to fund a crusade.</note>. In this Compendium or art, we have determined by a perfect declaration the outcome of the entire practice of this art, and the method of operating, through certain and effective instru-

# THE CODICIL

-ments <note>This completes the word "instrumenta" from the previous page.</note>, which are the three principal types of principles in this art. For when the practitioner <term>artifex: the alchemist or "maker" who works with natural processes</term>, who busies himself judging the internal effects of nature by means of external and unproven principles—which are in no way dependent on the art, since they are so far removed from it—he cannot gain a reliable knowledge of such things. This is especially true since he ought to proceed by a certain estimation <note>original: "æstimationem," referring to a reasoned judgment or expert calculation</note> that is close to the truth, as far as is possible for human reason, to penetrate the secrets of nature. That, however, which guides the practitioner's judgment so that he may approach the truth, is the rule of the Art's venerable and intellectual teaching. And because in such matters judgment is always regulated by the Art, it is therefore considered "of the Art" <note>original: "artificialis," meaning it pertains to the specialized skill and methodology of alchemy rather than being haphazard.</note>, which the powers of this Art demonstrate most clearly.

<margin>Otherwise: the matter.</margin>

->OF RAMON LLULL<-

A iij

<margin>Otherwise: from certain [principles].</margin>
...no art can exist unless it begins from its stated principles, some of which are material and exist in a state of being more or less purified; according to this degree of purity, they are either nearer to or further from the work, as we believe you, dearest [son], are not ignorant. Therefore, at the urging of the illustrious King Edward <note>Traditionally identified as Edward II of England, whom alchemical legend claims was a patron of Llull.</note>, into whose custody and divine protection this has been placed—lest such a great memory or knowledge of Philosophy be bestowed upon the wicked—we commend this work under a strict bond of secrecy, for the conversion of pagans and the preservation of the faith, upon which the salvation of the faithful depends. He is known to preside over this faith not only for the benefit of the body, but so that it may redound to the eternal advantage of the mind and soul. 

And because all works of the mind—whether in acting, or in apprehending and judging more clearly and perfectly—are annexed to virtues and faculties, compared according to the natural and due course for the increase of the whole Christian Religion and the salvation of souls, they cannot be integrated in a general sense, as we have proven in the 26th distinction of the *Questionnaire* <term>Quæstionario: likely referring to Llull's "Quaestiones per artem demonstrativam," a work applying his logical system to various problems</term> of this art. Therefore, by this consideration, the height of the royal crown is exalted in no small measure, since by this, an ample [provision] is openly provided for the mother of all the faithful <note>A reference to the Church.</note> in procreating...

Therefore, in this present brief discourse—to the honor of the heavenly Son and of the most happy and blessed Virgin Mary His mother, whose benevolence we have seen reach those who stand by with their hearts in every useful fullness of truth—it is our aim, with a humble gaze toward the common benefit of those who study Philosophy <note>original: "Philosophantium". ->*On the material principles of the first kind*<-

### CHAPTER V.

Therefore, the material principles usually follow the same matter of nature through a covenant of love <note>original: "amoris foedere." A common alchemical metaphor describing the natural attraction or chemical affinity between substances.</note> which nature herself, by her own proper, natural, and amorous actions, converts into a metallic form or an intermediate one. In the first order of the material principle, there are four practical principles, of which two are natural, and the remaining two are against nature <note>original: "contra naturam." This usually refers to "man-made" or "artificial" substances produced by the chemist that do not occur in that specific state in the wild.</note>. The natural ones are sulfur and mercury <note>original: "argentum viuum," literally "living silver" or quicksilver.</note>, and from these two a "metallic love" is born. Through this bond, the generation of the sought-after thing <note>original: "rei petitæ," referring to the intended alchemical goal, such as the Philosopher's Stone or purified metal.</note> occurs, when their own individual parts are gathered together in a natural covenant: that is, when their power is aided over the contrary...

<summary>Lull categorizes alchemical principles into four types—two natural (sulfur and mercury) and two "against nature"—explaining that the "love" or attraction between sulfur and mercury is what generates metallic forms.</summary>

# RAMON LLULL

...the power of that which is corruptible, as we shall immediately demonstrate. Against nature, however, are the nature of <term>vitriol<note>original: "vitrioli." A group of sulfate minerals (like copper or iron sulfate) used as powerful acidic reagents in alchemical work.</note></term> and the nature of <term>common salt<note>original: "salis communis." Sodium chloride; used in alchemical processes to help purify or separate metals.</note></term>, from which an attraction <note>original: "amor," literally "love." Alchemists used this term to describe chemical affinity or the natural tendency of substances to combine.</note> of another kind arises, though it is closely related in generation. With these substances, the corruption of the sought-after thing <note>original: "rei petitæ." The intended goal of the work, such as the Philosopher's Stone or purified gold.</note> occurs—specifically, insofar as their inferior nature is bolstered over the better nature of the <term>sulfur<note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter.</note></term> and <term>mercury<note>original: "argenti viui," literally "living silver." Mercury represents the passive, fluid, and feminine principle of matter.</note></term>. This frequently happens by the power of an excessive, muddy mixture that overcomes the matter of the sulfur and mercury; or it occurs from a defect in the preparation or the ignorance of the craftsman.

Therefore, so that your Royal Majesty <note>A reference to King Edward III of England, to whom this work was traditionally (though likely pseudepigraphally) dedicated.</note> may not be able to claim any cause of ignorance in that preparation, nor rebuke us in any way hereafter, we shall grant this knowledge to you—as it is most fruitful—just as we promised once the necessary obligations were met. 

It must first be understood, therefore, how from the principles already mentioned, several other principles are formed; and how from their nature, the aforementioned material principles descend successively and are gradually altered through known and certain operations. These are directed and governed by practical principles by means of intellectual doctrine, which are regulated by demonstrative principles. Fur-

...receiving into itself the form of sulfur or of metal, according as the power of one—<margin>Or rather, they are tempered one with another</margin>—overcomes the power of the other through mutual tempering; this is shown by the nature of metals, as they are led into a more noble form for all to see. Nor should you understand the aforementioned <term>sulfur<note>original: "sulphur." In this context, it refers to the "active" principle of matter that provides form and stability.</note></term> to be created as such in its entire substance by nature within its own mine. ## CHAPTER VI.

The general demonstrative principles by which the artist, being distinguished, provides artificial form to the truth that is sought,

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "demonstrative principles" introduced in Chapter VI. It moves from the theoretical definitions of sulfur and mercury on the previous page to the observable physical changes—specifically the color sequence—that a practitioner must monitor to ensure the work is proceeding correctly.</meta>

-># CODICILLUS<-

These signs are of a more permanent character, released in succession from the material foundations during the <term>decoctions<note>original: "decoctionibus." In alchemy, this refers to the prolonged process of heating, boiling, or refining a substance in a sealed vessel.</note></term>. These are the four principal colors: <term>blackness<note>original: "nigredo." The first stage, representing the breakdown and purification of the "prime matter."</note></term>, <term>whiteness<note>original: "albedo." The second stage, signifying that the matter has been purified.</note></term>, <term>yellowness<note>original: "citrinatio." A transitional stage of ripening or "gold-making."</note></term>, and <term>redness<note>original: "rubedo." The final stage, representing the ultimate perfection and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term>. From these arise as many accidental mixed colors as can be imagined in all of nature, yet they do not persist in their effect; only the four essential colors mentioned above remain as the guides for the whole <term>mastery<note>original: "magisterij." This refers to the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus), the successful completion of the alchemical process.</note></term>.

Of these, the first color—blackness—concludes in white; then the white concludes in yellow, and the yellow in red. This redness is the final demonstrative principle and the goal of perfection for the entire work. For this reason, these principles were given along with certain other specific details mentioned here and in the practical application. Thus, through the knowledge of these signs, the careful <term>artist<note>original: "artista." In this context, it refers to the alchemist as a skilled practitioner of the "Sacred Art."</note></term> may know how to manage what he observes from nature through demonstrable signs in practice. By following the established rule of the art, he will know how to govern his selected matter, which must be properly shaped according to the pattern of nature by the practical principles that follow.

<summary>This page defines the four essential color stages—black, white, yellow, and red—that act as visible indicators of a successful alchemical transformation. It emphasizes that while many fleeting colors may appear, these four are the permanent markers that guide the alchemist toward the final perfection of the Work.</summary>

## On the practical principles of the third kind
## and those things which flow from them.

### CHAPTER VII.

The principal operative principles of the third kind in this art are the four <term>converting virtues<note>original: "virtutes conuertentes." These are the primary actions used to transform the substance from one state to another.</note></term>: namely, **to dissolve**, **to wash**, **to restore**, and **to fix**. From each of these depend many other specific practical principles regarding the management of <term>fires<note>original: "ignium." In alchemy, "fire" refers not just to a flame, but to the degree and type of heat applied to the vessel.</note></term> and their specific qualities: for example, the natural, the unnatural, the "against nature," and the artificial, as will be shown briefly through the successive operations. Therefore, in explaining each of these—while in no way departing from practical experience—we shall set them forth in the shortest and easiest way possible. We will do this alongside the two principal movements of nature, namely <term>corruption<note>original: "corruptionis." The process of breaking down a substance so it can be reborn in a new form.</note></term> and <term>generation<note>original: "generationis." The process of creating or "birthing" the new, perfected substance.</note></term>, so that the <term>son of the art<note>original: "artis filius." A traditional term for a dedicated student or practitioner of alchemy.</note></term> may more readily and easily grasp the effective method through the aforementioned instruments. Once these principles are understood, as well as the practice that follows from them and the method of operating, let the <term>artist<note>original: "artifex." The alchemist performing the work.</note></term> return to the nature of the natural, unnatural, and "against nature" mediums, so that he may know how to govern according to...

<summary>This page introduces the four fundamental alchemical operations—dissolving, washing, restoring, and fixing—and emphasizes the importance of mastering different types of heat (fires) to achieve transformation.</summary>
<keywords>converting virtues, dissolve, wash, restore, fix, types of fire, corruption and generation, son of the art, alchemical practice</keywords>

### *On corruption and generation.*
## CHAPTER VIII.

The integrity of this <term>mastery<note>original: "magisterij." This refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy—the complete process of perfecting a substance.</note></term> considers two movements of the principles, by which <term>corruption<note>original: "corruptio." In alchemical terms, this is the necessary decay or breakdown of a substance before it can be reborn in a higher form.</note></term> and <term>generation<note>original: "generatio." The creation or "birth" of a new, perfected substance following the decay of the old one.</note></term> are signaled: namely, the **natural** and the **unnatural**. 

The **unnatural movement** depends on the movement of the whole matter, and very much upon the evacuation of the <term>spirits<note>original: "spiritibus." These are the volatile, gaseous components of a substance that can be driven out by heat.</note></term> from the body. The **natural movement** arises from the agitation of heat within the body. 

For this reason, when a person travels in the summer after eating, the <term>natural heat<note>original: "calor naturalis." The internal heat of a living body or an alchemical vessel that drives maturation; it is compared here to the biological process of digestion.</note></term> within the body is ignited after the meal, and the movement of perfect <term>digestion<note>original: "digestionis." Like biological digestion, alchemical digestion involves the slow, steady application of heat to ripen a substance.</note></term> is not only slowed down, but rather becomes confused, leading to the body's corruption. This happens because the spirits are dissolved and the natural heat is diminished. Because of this, people who travel extensively are commonly thin, appearing like <term>choleric<note>original: "cholerici." A temperament associated with yellow bile—hot and dry—tending toward irritability and a lean build.</note></term> and <term>melancholic<note>original: "melancholici." A temperament associated with black bile—cold and dry—tending toward sadness and a thin frame.</note></term> types. This movement is formed from the first practical principle, yet it occurs with <term>unnatural fire<note>original: "igne innaturali." Heat that is too intense or external, which can disrupt the delicate internal balance of the work.</note></term>, through other principles...

...the conservation of nature: because natural motion is not entirely confounded, just as it is not advantageous [to do so], as was said regarding unnatural motion. Rather, by repairing the old individual substances, which were in some way corrupted, lead them back into a new state.

This, moreover, is what we have stated: namely, that <term>unnatural motion<note>original: "innaturalis motus." The principle that the essential form or identity of a substance must be preserved throughout its chemical transformations.</note></term>. For all natural things possess a contrary quality by which they exceed their bounds—not in their essential power, but in a specific way—leading toward the breakdown of individual things. 

This breakdown does not go all the way back to <term>prime matter<note>original: "materiam primam." The formless, universal "base" of all reality which has no specific qualities.</note></term>, but to that <term>proximate matter<note>original: "materiam proximam." The immediate raw material that is already specific to a certain family of things (like the "seed" of metals) and thus easier for the alchemist to transform.</note></term> closest to the metallic genus, in which there is a greater likeness to the natural agent than in that other, first matter. Because of this, if it were to pass beyond this "proximate matter," the greatness of its likeness would be lost, and consequently, its balance and lesser qualities. And so, in the natural course...

**intermediates**<note>original: "mediis." Continued from the previous page's final word fragment.</note> is assisted by the power of another. In every natural science, many intermediates exist, and the knowledge of them is necessary in all transmutations. Regarding this, you will have certainty of knowledge through the exquisite power of their nature—whether they be material or operative, or demonstrable signs—by looking to the nature of the genus upon which each of them immediately or absolutely depends according to its final act. <margin>Otherwise, for example.</margin> **Sulphur** and **mercury**<note>original: "argentum viuum." Literally "living silver." In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid, metallic, and feminine principle, while Sulphur represents the combustible, active, and masculine principle.</note> are the extremes of all things; the metals between them will be the intermediates. 

But metals [differ from mercury in composition, in the same way that mercury itself, through purity and impurity, differs from the foul, earthy sulphur that is foreign to it.] For this reason, two are found to be more purified than the others, namely **Gold** and **Silver**: without which this art cannot be completed, since in them is the purest substance of sulphur, perfectly purified by the ingenuity of nature. Art is weaker than nature regarding this purification, nor is it able to achieve it, even though it labors much in the task. In the art, therefore, it is said: **Its father is the Sun, its mother is the Moon**<note>A famous maxim from the *Emerald Tablet* of Hermes Trismegistus. In this context, the Sun represents Gold and the Moon represents Silver.</note>: because from those two bodies with their sulphur—

# CODICILLVS

**bodies**<note>original: "corpore" (fragmented from previous page). This refers to the metallic bodies of gold and silver mentioned previously.</note> or prepared from arsenic, our **Medicine**<note>original: "medicina." In alchemy, the "Medicine" refers to the Elixir or the Philosopher's Stone, which was believed to "cure" base metals of their imperfections.</note> can be elicited, and without them it cannot be had. They are called **intermediates**<note>original: "media."</note>, however, because they are naturally composed of the first pure extremes, namely of **sulphur** and **mercury**<note>original: "argentum viuo," literally "living silver."</note>: and they are neither sulphur, nor mercury, but intermediates between them, since mercury, by its own property and the vapor of its purified sulphur, is congealed into a perfect metal. 

And according as the said metals possess more of the nature of mercury, they are of greater perfection, such as gold and silver: and according as they possess less of the nature of mercury, and more of the nature of sulphur, they are of greater corruption: such as the other imperfect metals: and especially **marcasite**<term>marcassita: a general alchemical term for various sulfide minerals, often iron pyrites (fool's gold)</term>, **magnesia**<term>magnesia: in this period, likely referring to manganese or magnesium-rich ores used to refine glass or metals</term>, and **tutty**<term>tuthia: crude zinc oxide collected from the chimneys of smelting furnaces</term>: and beyond these, of even greater corruption are **salts**, **alums**, **vitriols**<term>atramenta: literally "blackings," referring to metallic sulfates used in making inks and dyes</term>, and others which are called intermediates between stone and metal. 

We say, moreover, that the extremes, insofar as they are of their nearby <margin>Otherwise, by those most near.</margin> intermediates, are continually drawn out effectively from their own virtues, and according to the reason of following nature, they are deemed to be retained for their own proper action. Furthermore, my son, you must successively take up the alterable and miscible principles...

Lull explains that a metal's perfection depends on its mercury content, while higher sulphur content leads to "corruption" or imperfection.</summary>

## RAYMOND LULL

...are more suited for alteration by reason of the proximity of their kindred nature, which in them is closer and more perfect. For example:
<margin>Otherwise called H.</margin>
**B** is the principle for **G** to attain **M** by means of **G**; and **G** is the principle for **M** to attain **R** through **M**. And **M** is the principle for **R** so that through it one can reach **Y** step-by-step<note>original: "gradatim." Lull’s alchemy is famously "graduated," meaning it proceeds through a strictly defined hierarchy of stages.</note>, and be converted into its nature. And thus it is clear how the nature of the extremes<note>In alchemy, "extremes" usually refer to the starting material and the final goal, such as a base metal and gold.</note> is knowable through the nature of their intermediates. And conversely, because **G** holds the nature of the medium of conjunction for **B** and **M**; and **M** retains the medium of conjunction of the nature through which **G** is turned into **R**; and **R** is the nature akin to the medium through which the nature of **M** is converted into the nature of **Y**.<note>Lull uses an "Alphabet" of letters (B, G, M, R, Y) as variables to represent different substances and stages in his logical-alchemical system, allowing him to demonstrate transmutations as if they were mathematical or logical proofs.</note>

And by **B**, you should understand the **primary material principles**, namely **gold** and **silver**, as well as the nature of **glass** and **salt** with **mercury**. By **G**, you should understand the **secondary material principles**, namely the **elements**—**earth**, **water**, **air**, and **fire**—which, by successive and graded operations, have flowed and been elicited from the aforementioned primary material principles. And by **M**, you should understand the **tertiary material principles**, which are **sulphur** and pure **quicksilver**<note>original: "argentum viuum."</note>, and the **ferments of the Philosophers**<term>fermenta Philosophorum: substances (often highly refined gold or silver) added to the alchemical "Stone" to give it the power to transform other metals, acting like yeast in dough.</term>, successively...

The "unnatural fire," however, is the medium between the two. He who has fixed the form of this example or rule in his mind, and has diligently followed it, will infallibly know their qualities. 

Therefore, let the artisan consider the natural powers and strengths of the <term>Philosophers' Stones</term> <note>term: <term>Philosophers' Stones</term> (lapidum Philosophorum). He must understand what they are and what kind they are in the whole course of nature, and which of them is a friend or an enemy to the others. ### In what ways and to what extent the Stone can be strengthened and weakened, and concerning the substance of its power.

## CHAPTER 11.

We shall explain in how many ways the **power** <note>original: *virtus*. In this context, it refers to the active potency or "virtue" of the alchemical substance.</note> of the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> can be strengthened, and by which ways it can be weakened. For in as many ways as one of a pair of opposites is defined, the other is defined in just as many ways, as is notably understood through the **science of means and extremes** <note>A logical principle where understanding one pole (like strength) helps define its opposite (weakness) and the states in between.</note>. The power of the stone is weakened, in general, in two ways.

The first is when the substance of **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: *Argentum vivum* or mercury. In alchemy, this is not just the common metal, but the essential fluid principle that carries the "life" or "heat" of the work.</term>—which carries the power within the body—is diminished through weakness; specifically, the subject of **natural heat** <note>original: *caloris naturalis*. A vital, maturing force believed to be contained within matter, necessary for alchemical transformation.</note>. This substance, in which the power of heat resides, is called "quicksilver" by the **Philosopher** <note>Likely referring to Aristotle, who was frequently cited simply as "The Philosopher" in medieval scholarly and alchemical texts.</note>, and is sometimes called the "essence of the body" or the "essence of quicksilver": because through it the Stone has its actual existence, and without it, it does not.

There are, therefore, two subjects of this power <margin>another reading: namely, the body which is the subject of the innate [power].</margin> [namely, that of the body and that of the spirit. Because the subject of the body is called the "subject of nature," and the subject of the spirit is called the "subject of the **flowing**" <note>original: *fluentis*. This refers to the volatile or fluidic aspect of the substance, as opposed to the fixed or solid body.</note>.] Therefore, when the substance of the body or the spirit is diminished, it neces—

For since this art proceeds by true degrees and must be investigated from the secrets of **nature**—without which the practical application of the whole of **medicine** cannot be made complete—you will fundamentally and fully comprehend this art under its established principles. He is truly beloved by God, to whom this [knowledge is granted]...

# THE LITTLE CODICIL #

This likely refers to Lull's other major alchemical work, the *Testamentum*, which served as a companion guide to his practical experiments.</note> not be far from you, because it opens and reveals the hidden recesses of this theory along with its certain and known operations. Therefore, set your heart toward its **practice**.

## *On the division of this mastery.*

### C H A P T E R XII.

This mastery <term>Mastery: *magisterium*. The completion of the alchemical process, resulting in the Philosopher's Stone or the "Great Elixir."</term> is divided into four parts, according to the four practical principles. The first part shows the way of making the coarse refined <note>original: *graciliandi grossum*. This refers to thinning or purifying a dense substance.</note> through liquefaction, elementation, dissolution, and the separation of the stone into the four elements. The second part shows the way of lightening the heavy through distillations, calcinations, rarefactions, condensations, burials <term>Burial: *inhumatio*. A technique where a sealed vessel is placed in warm earth, ash, or fermenting horse dung to provide a gentle, constant heat.</term>, congelations, solutions, and animations, which are the preparations of the elements. <margin>Otherwise: separations.</margin> The third part shows the way of quickening by the spirit <note>original: *vivificandi à spiritu*. In alchemy, "spirit" refers to the volatile or gaseous state of a substance.</note>, and the nourishment of the stone, through inhibitions, decoctions, roastings, burials, and sublimations of the stone. <margin>Otherwise: lightening the rough.</margin> The fourth part shows the way of sweetening the bitter <note>The original Latin *perum* is likely a contraction or error for *purum* or *ipsum*, modifying "bitter."</note> and ripening the raw, through reductions, impregnations, burials, fomentations, animations, fixations, and incerations <term>Inceration: *inceratio*. The process of adding a liquid to a dry substance until it reaches a soft, wax-like consistency.</term>. If, therefore, you make the coarse refined through dissolution, and you make the heavy light through smoothing or rarefaction, and the rough smooth through reductio-

->### CHAPTER XIII<-

Since it is established, as was said above, that the art must be begun from its certain and determined principles, one must therefore return to those, as they are the effective causes of practical fullness. A specific stage or set of operations in the alchemical process governed by a particular "rule" or method.</term> cannot be perfected—just as a middle <margin>Otherwise: as if through.</margin> cannot be perfected without its extremes <note>original: *extremis*. In alchemical logic, the "extremes" are the starting material and the final result; the "middle" is the transformative process connecting them.</note> by which it must be integrated—therefore, by declaring from them in both mystic and open speech, the Philosopher <note>original: *Philosophus*. A title usually reserved for an authority like Aristotle, though in this context it refers to the collective wisdom of the alchemical masters.</note> relates that one in no way differs from the other in terms of the artificial practice and process, which shall be described briefly and in order. The first major stage of the Great Work, symbolizing the decomposition or "death" of the substance, which must occur before it can be purified and reborn.</term>, without which the craft cannot be successfully begun.

### *Concerning the first practical principle, and how Mercury is purified through the first sublimation with prepared common Salt and vitriol: and infuse the Mercury in such water, etc.*

->CHAPTER XIV<-

The purification of Mercury <term>Mercury: *Mercurius*. In alchemy, this refers both to the chemical element and the "spirit" or volatile principle of all metals.</term> through the first sublimation is performed by taking one part of D <note>D: In Llull’s symbolic alphabet, 'D' often signifies a mineral agent, such as vitriol or a specific acidic salt used as a solvent.</note> and half a part of C <note>C: In Llull's system, 'C' typically represents Salt or the "Body" being acted upon.</note> and distilling the water according to that method which nature requires, as we have explained in our *Testament* <note>original: *testamento*. Referring to Llull’s *Testamentum*, a seminal alchemical text that uses an algebraic-style code to describe chemical recipes.</note>, in the chapter that begins: *You, by the virtue of A, etc.* And infuse into such water the Mercury—specifically the "testamentary" Mercury <note>testamentary Mercury: A specific preparation of mercury described in the aforementioned *Testament*.</note>. Then separate all the water through sublimation <term>Sublimation: *sublimatio*. A process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then condensed back into a purified solid, leaving impurities behind.</term>. Afterward, sublime it until it is as white as crystal. Moreover, we hope in our Lord Jesus Christ that it shall yet be sublimated to greater and more lofty things through the sublime degrees of preparation, with the addition of the substance that tints <note>original: *rei tingentis*. The "tincture" or agent of transmutation that gives the metal its final color and quality.</note> it, and its soul shall be exalted in glory. Therefore, with three ounces of such Mercury, add an equal amount of the purest Sun <term>Sun: *Sol*. ->RAYMOND LLULL<-

It is in the manner of white salt with its own viscosity, which is in the manner of a blackish oil; its moisture could not be separated by water, for reasons expressed in our *Testament* <note>Llull refers here to his major work, the *Testamentum*, a foundational text of pseudo-Lullian alchemy which details the theoretical framework of the art.</note>, in the chapter concerning the animation of the elements, and elsewhere regarding their rarefaction <term>Rarefaction: *rarefactio*. See, my son, what you do in this step, and beware lest the material turns red by excessive heat in the beginning, and the tinctures <term>Tinctures: *tincturae*. To heat a substance to a high temperature (but below its melting point) in order to drive off volatile fractions and reduce it to a powder.</term> the body without moisture, it will be turned red, and the property of the sulfur <term>Sulfur: *sulphuris*. In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter, responsible for color and hardness.</term> will be burned: <margin>Otherwise it may retain it.</margin> since it has no tether <note>original: *retinaculum*. A "restraint" or "holdfast" that keeps the volatile spirits bound to the fixed body.</note> to carry it in its belly and defend it from combustion. 

Therefore, defend it from combustion by placing some of its own moisture with it before the redness comes, and apply to it the heat of iron filings <note>original: *ferratura*. Iron filings were used in sand baths to distribute heat evenly and maintain a specific, intense but steady temperature.</note>, extracting the moisture, little by little drying out the material: because thus...

change from color to color during that 
putrefaction <term>Putrefaction: *putrefactio*. In alchemy, this is the essential stage of "death" or decomposition, where the old form of the metal is broken down so it can be reborn in a perfected state.</term>, namely into greenness, 
which is one of the strange signs 
leading from the first principle toward the white stage. 
In the red stage, however, blackness 
will appear after the yellowing <note>original: *citrinitatem*. Citrinitas (yellowness) was traditionally a transitional stage between the white and red "works" or perfections of the stone.</note>. 
Because when the body is emptied of 
its moist spirits, it suffers in such a way 
that it is transmuted from color to color. 
Protect it, therefore, always from combustion, 
lest the natural heat be suffocated 
in any part, and the property of the Mercury 
be scorched: namely, that it should be 
cooked by roasting <note>original: *assando*. Roasting refers to dry, direct heating, as opposed to the "moist" heat of a water bath.</note> only as much as the 
dissolution can tolerate, and no more: 
because it would immediately turn red, 
and as much as the body loses in 
moisture by roasting, it must be 
dissolved by as much through imbibing <term>Imbibing: *imbibendo*. The process of slowly adding liquid—usually the "mercurial water"—back to the dried-out solid matter to nourish it and encourage growth.</term>. 
For nature does this, desiring to recover 
what was lost, just as we have expressly 
noted in the theory of our *Testament*, 
and will fully demonstrate below 
by way of practice. When, therefore, you see 
the body is dry—if for the white work, 
under the color of green; if for the red 
work, under the color of black— 
withdraw the fire and allow it 
to cool: once cooled, pour the water 
over the dry earth, and immediately 
place it in the bath <note>The catchword "neo" on the original manuscript completes the word *balneo* (bath), referring to the *balneum Mariae* or water bath used for gentle, indirect heating.</note>

...for distilling the water, by receiving it through a well-continued and equal fire. Once it has been distilled, you will see at the bottom that the body suffered <note>original: *patiebatur*. ### *Concerning the instruction on repetition.*

## CHAPTER XVI.

<margin>
The
secret line
of dissolution
</margin>
Son, you shall repeat all these operations turn after turn for three times, or four if you wish, so that the water may be elementalized through such operations, and some of the body’s own substance may be diffused into it, and the body may be broken down into its smallest parts <note>original: *per minima*. This refers to the concept of "minima naturalia," the alchemical precursor to the idea of atoms or molecules—breaking a substance down to its most basic, indivisible components.</note>, and its property may be increased within the water, so that afterward it may be converted into its own nature through attraction. Therefore, reiterate the aforementioned natural grindings, imbibitions <term>Imbibitions: *imbibitiones*. The process of saturating a dry substance with liquid, often compared to "feeding" the matter.</term> and dissolutions, and decoctions <term>Decoctions: *decoctiones*. The process of boiling or heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or "cook" it to perfection.</term>, and you will be on the line of secret dissolution.

### *Concerning the second union and elementalization, and first concerning the distinction of the alphabetic letters.*

## CHAPTER XVII.

<margin>

To this distinction of letters, we wish to refer the fact that **V.** signifies the heat of the secret furnace <note>original: *furni secreti*. This refers to a specialized alchemical furnace, often designed for constant, regulated heat over long periods.</note> and all its properties: which are attenuation <term>Attenuation: *attenuatio*. The process of making a substance thinner or less dense.</term>, attraction <note>original: *attractatio*. In this context, it refers to the drawing in of substances or energies during the heating process.</note>, inspiration, simple vivification <term>Vivification: *vivificatio*. "Quickening" or bringing a "dead" chemical substance back to a state of activity or "life."</term>, inhumation <note>original: *ihumatio*. The practice of burying a sealed vessel in warm dung or earth to maintain a gentle, even temperature.</note>, and the conjunction of spirit and body without division. And by **X.**, we wish to signify the dry fire of ashes <note>original: *ignis siccus cinerum*. An ash-bath, or *sand-bath*, where the vessel is placed in a container of ashes or sand heated from below to provide indirect heat.</note>, whether from ironwork or from charcoal, and its other properties: which are mortification <note>original: *mortificatio*. The "killing" or chemical alteration of a metal to strip it of its outward form.</note>, calcination, rarefaction, the retention of the spirit, and separation. By **Y.**, we wish to signify the heat of the bath <note>original: *calor balnei*. The *balneum Mariae* or "Mary’s bath" (bain-marie), which uses boiling water to provide a steady heat of 100°C.</note> and all its properties, which are elementalization, rarefaction, vivification, washing, distillation, animation <term>Animation: *animatio*. The process of "infusing a soul" into a substance, usually by recombining a distilled "spirit" with its "body."</term>, and the separation of spirits. By **Z.**, we wish to understand no other operation except the separation of the liquid and the substance of the entire liquefied body. It is necessary that you know the meanings of these letters with all their properties by heart <note>original: *corde tenus*. Literally "to the extent of the heart."</note>, because otherwise the course of nature [cannot be] well followed...

...you will not be able to work or practice, nor will you know how to properly arrange the properties of that operation, unless [you do so] through the known power of those letters <note>original: *literarum illarum*. ## *Concerning rarefaction and its thinning.*

### CHAPTER XVIII.

The rarefaction <term>Rarefaction: *rarefactio*. The process of making a substance thinner, more volatile, or less dense—essentially turning a liquid into a vapor or a more "spiritual" state.</term> of this water is achieved through a double distillation in a bath <note>original: *balneo*. Short for *balneum Mariae* (Mary's bath), a water bath used to provide gentle, even heat to a vessel.</note> by itself and without the body <note>original: *sine corpore*. In alchemy, the "body" usually refers to the solid, metallic, or fixed portion of a substance.</note>. Distill it, therefore, twice with a very slow and well-sustained fire; however, collect the dregs <note>original: *feces*. The solid, impure residue left at the bottom of a vessel after distillation.</note> which it produces in each distillation and place them with the earth calcinated <term>Calcinated: *calcinata*. A substance reduced to a dry, crumbly powder through the application of high heat, intended to "fix" it or remove impurities.</term> above.

Literally "alien fire," referring to an external heat source that is too intense or improperly regulated for the natural progression of the alchemical stages.</term>.

...are signified: and the more you grind <term>Grind: *teres*. The manual labor of using a mortar and pestle to break down solid matter.</term>, the more you will soften the material. In alchemy, the volatile or gaseous essence of a substance.</term> are then made into a paste <term>Paste: *impastantur*. An alchemical process of gradually adding liquid to a dry substance until it reaches the consistency of soft wax.</term>, and roasting <term>Roasting: *assatione*. Because nature, through these grindings and roastings, divides the bound parts by means of the viscosity of the water which is in the bodies, and the material is resolved from them into the smallest particles. Consequently, the dissolved bodies are reduced to the nature of the spirit, even though they are more fixed <term>Fixed: *fixa*. Refers to a substance that has become stable and will no longer evaporate or fly away when exposed to high heat.</term>. This spirit, however, partakes of two extremes in nature, as if holding within itself both spirituality and corporality, fixation and volatility. Therefore, if its parts are thickened in any way by fire, it does not allow itself to be corrupted further; nor, when a raging flame enters it, does it allow itself to be raised up into smoke. This is because it does not suffer its own thinning <note>original: *rarefactionem*. Here meaning the loss of density or the act of turning into a gas.</note> on account of its density and its lack of "burning" <note>original: *adustionis*. This refers to the capacity of a substance to be scorched, charred, or consumed by fire.</note>, a quality produced by the sulfurous nature <term>Sulfurous nature: *sulphuritatem*. In the alchemical tradition, sulfur was seen as the principle of combustibility and "oiliness."</term> which it does not possess. And so, when it is fixed, the tincture <term>Tincture: *tinctura*. The potent, concentrated essence produced at the end of the work, capable of transforming or "coloring" other substances.</term> is re[d] <margin>Otherwise: of whiteness <note>original: *Alias albedinis*. This marginal note indicates that a different manuscript version describes the result as white (*albedo*) rather than red (*rubedo*).</note></margin>...

...dyeing <note>Dyeing: *tingens*. In alchemy, "tincture" refers to the ability of a substance to transform or "color" the properties of another material, often associated with the final stages of the Work.</note> most perfectly, and it does not withdraw from the mixture, just as water does not withdraw when mixed with water. For "nature rejoices in nature" <note>original: "natura naturæ lætatur". A foundational alchemical maxim suggesting that substances of a similar essence combine harmoniously and effectively.</note>, when the groom is joined to the bride. <margin>Homogeneity</margin> Moreover, since it is entirely airy in nature, it either remains entirely in the fire, or it withdraws entirely with its substance into smoke, as it is incombustible and airy—which is a sign of perfection. If you mix it into sulfurous earth when it is heated, it will ascend and be sublimated <term>Sublimated: *sublimabitur*. The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then re-collects as a solid on a cooler surface, used to purify the material.</term>. And if you repeat this sublimation with "Philosophic continuity" <note>This refers to the steady, uninterrupted application of heat over a long period, which was considered essential for the maturation of the alchemical Stone.</note> over its own earth, it will be thoroughly purified, cooked <term>Cooked: *decoquetur*. Also known as decoction; the application of slow heat to refine or thicken a substance.</term>, and thickened <term>Thickened: *inspissabitur*. The process of making a liquid more solid through evaporation or heat.</term>, and it will gradually congeal into white and red sulfur <note>These represent the two key stages of the alchemical "Great Work"—the *albedo* (whiteness) and the *rubedo* (redness), signaling the final perfection of the material.</note>. <margin>Alternative reading: But those parts which are not dissolved have no way to be refined unless, etc. Alternative: From which it is clear that you need it as in the solution.</margin> **Dissolve the bodies, therefore, and you will find what you seek.** Because when the parts within the whole are not dissolved, there is nothing that can be refined, unless you soften them. [Therefore, when you labor in the repetition of the solution, you separate the purer parts from the impure: because that part which does not agree with its whole is always subject to foulness.] Separate, therefore, those parts which are heavier by nature, so that once they are cast aside, you may complete the work with the lighter parts, using that same method we described above in chapter 23: "Afterward," etc., and in similar chapters regarding the stone’s...

If indeed any part of the dry and coarse body still remains to be liquefied and **quickened** <term>Quickening (Vivification): The alchemical process of "bringing to life" a solid metal by transforming it into a fluid, active state capable of further change.</term>, then the **wheel of the elements' conversion** <term>Wheel of the Elements: A fundamental concept in Lullian alchemy where the four elements—Earth, Water, Air, Fire—are rotated and transformed into one another to achieve a perfect, balanced state.</term> is not yet perfect. Unless this wheel is perfected, the structure or "machine" of this entire principle will be utterly destroyed. For no active agent, even when approaching the likeness of a natural physical form, can perfect the matter unless it renders the whole of it **homogeneous** <term>Homogeneous: Consisting of parts all of the same kind; in this context, it means the substance has become a single, uniform "medicine" without any undigested or "coarse" remnants.</term>. This is achieved through the figure of the first wheel of the mutual conversion of the elements, just as we have fully declared in our *Testament* <note>Referring to Lully's major work, the "Testamentum," which serves as a foundation for the "Codicillus."</note>, and here as well.

## CHAPTER XXIX.

Now it is necessary to empty the **body** <term>Body: In alchemy, the "body" refers to the solid, physical matter or metallic base being worked upon.</term>—which has remained on the path of its perfection—of its **spirits** <term>Spirits: The volatile components of a substance that can be vaporized or "lifted" by heat, as opposed to the solid "body."</term>, so that it may be **calcined** <term>Calcined: Reduced to a fine powder or ash through intense heat to purify or "open" the material.</term> and **mortified** <term>Mortification: The process of "killing" the metallic form or density of a substance to prepare it for a new, higher state of existence.</term>. This is done so that it may recover within itself a greater **rarity** <note>original: "raritatem"; here meaning the substance becomes less dense or more porous.</note>, through which it may attract a greater humidity; for rarer things attract greater humidity, even to the very depths of the altered body. For this reason, take that substance and treat it in operation according to the method described in the chapters designated by **V**.

### CHAPTER XXXII.

**S**on, the effect and intention of this regime <term>Regimen: In alchemy, this refers to the "government" of the fire and the specific stages of the chemical process.</term> is that the powers of mercury <term>Argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the metal mercury, which alchemists believed was the mother of all metals.</term> should dominate over the powers of sulfur, and by the power of the one they undergo the influence of the powers of the other, and it converts that substance into its own nature. In this, art imitates nature. <margin>Otherwise: convertible. Otherwise: let it be converted into the substance of sulfur and be congealed.</margin> 

However, hold this in memory within the chamber of your mind <note>original: *cellula mentis*; a common Medieval philosophical term for the specific parts of the brain where memory and thought were believed to reside.</note>: it is necessarily required that the body, which is the principal converting agent, must first be converted into a convertible spirit, so that through that convertible spirit it may be congealed into the substance of that sulfur. And for this reason, we recall having said in our Testament that no mercury is congealed; rather, it is converted into the substance of sulfur.

### *Concerning the penultimate practice, which is about the putrefaction of the elements converted into one another, and joined into one.*

## CHAPTER XXXIII.

<margin>Elsewhere it is said that it should rot there for forty days.</margin>
Indeed, my son, the intention which you must have in this part is that you take the dissolved stone and do not delay in placing it in a secret furnace <insert>so that it stays there for four days.</insert> This is done so that the "burnings" and "martyrizations" <note>original: *martyrizationes*; a vivid alchemical term for the harsh chemical treatments, such as intense heat or corrosive acids, that "torture" the metal to strip away its impurities.</note> which the body suffered in the aforementioned stages from its animated liquid substances may be removed through "animation" and a gentle boiling <term>Decoction: The process of heating or boiling a substance in a liquid to extract its medicinal or chemical essences.</term>, and all that which is removed is restored to quicksilver <term>Argentum vivum: Literally "living silver," the metal mercury, which alchemists believed was the fluid "soul" of all metals.</term>. For the sulfurous elements, mixed with the watery parts of the quicksilver, having now been converted into their smallest particles <note>original: *per minima*; a concept in medieval physics referring to mixing substances at the most fundamental, microscopic level possible.</note>, are altered and likewise corrupted together. Such a formal corruption of our quicksilver is the generation <note>In alchemical philosophy, based on Aristotelian physics, the "corruption" (decay) of one form is necessary for the "generation" (birth) of another.</note> which cannot be had except after putrefaction <term>Putrefaction: The process of rotting; in alchemy, this stage is usually represented by the color black and signifies the "death" of the old metal before its rebirth as gold.</term>, which is the mother of all things. Because through this operation, the body naturally rots in the quicksilver, and its sulfurous qualities h... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with "have" or "are held."</note>

This is because the prime matter of all things that can be generated or destroyed is the same: uncreated and incorruptible. It is from this that many of the ancients assert that primordial matter has no form; and many others have said that it is **pure being in potentiality** <term>Ens purum in potentia: A philosophical concept describing something that has the capacity to become anything, but does not yet have a physical shape or identity.</term>. Nevertheless, it is a certain **universal agent** [in nature] by which it uniformly gathers its substances together in order to corrupt them and generate them anew. 

For this reason, it is in no way to be understood except through an **anagogical** <note>Anagogical: A method of interpretation that seeks a hidden spiritual or mystical meaning behind the literal words.</note> or analogical sense regarding the **universal form**, which, like something breathing, participates with the **fifth matter** <term>Res Quinta: The Quintessence or "fifth essence," the purest part of matter that remains after the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) are refined.</term>.

## *On the final practice of the first regimen, which concerns the division of elements connected to each other, and first concerning water.*

### CHAPTER XXXIV.

Therefore, let the above-mentioned putrefied compound be taken in the name of God, and through an <term>alembic</term> <note>A classic distilling apparatus consisting of a vessel for heating (the cucurbit) and a cap (the head) with a tube to carry off vapors.</note> first distill all the water in a <term>bain-marie</term> <note>original: *balneo mariæ*. Literally "Mary’s bath," a vessel of hot water used to provide gentle, uniform heat to a secondary container, preventing the substance from burning.</note>, continuing with a slow fire and equal distribution of heat, until by the power of that heat, the more subtle parts of the <term>stone</term> <note>original: *lapidis*. In this context, the "stone" refers to the alchemical matter or "Philosophers' Stone" in its developing stages.</note>—which approach the nature of simple wateriness alone without heat—have been distilled, so that nothing more can be distilled by that specific temperature. 

Now, the reason why we separate the elements is so that each one may be fully prepared on its own, according to the requirements of its nature, and so the stone may be made more <term>subtle</term> <note>In alchemy, "subtlety" refers to the refinement and purification of matter, making it less dense and more spiritually active.</note>, so that by reason of its refinement it may afterwards be more firmly joined together. Therefore, divide the elements through the first regimen, and afterwards <term>rectify</term> <note>original: *rectifica*. To purify a substance, often through repeated distillation or filtration, to reach its most potent state.</note> them through the second regimen.

## *On the separation of air and the mortification of the earth.*

### *CHAPTER XXXV.*

Son, let the simple air with its own simple fire be distilled through <term>ashes</term> <note>original: *cineres*. An ash-bath (balneum cineris) was used in the laboratory to provide a stronger, more even heat than a water bath, as ashes retain heat longer and reach higher temperatures.</note>, because they sustain a greater heating, and throughout your entire practice it will be clear that they sustain simple fieriness. And this is for this reason: because it is necessary for the heats and the coarser earthy parts to be raised upwards through simple heating. 

Take, therefore, the <term>distilling vessel</term> <note>original: *distillatorium*. A vessel used for heating and vaporizing a substance so its components can be separated.</note> with all the matter, and place it over the ashes, and distill the air to one side with a continuous fire made from well-prepared <term>sawdust</term> <note>original: *serratura*. Alchemists often used sawdust as a slow-burning fuel to maintain a steady, consistent temperature over long periods.</note>, until for this turn you have received all the air mixed with fire, and the fire hidden within the <term>depths</term> <note>original: *visceribus*. Literally "bowels" or "entrails," used metaphorically here to mean the innermost part of the element.</note> of the air. However, that which remains burnt at the bottom is the fire that is to be separated from the dry and black earth.

Because two of the elements are "stony"—namely, **fire** and **earth**, which agree in their dryness—they must be separated in such an order that the **air** or **fire** is not confused with the "earthy air" during their rectifications. And therefore, since the hotter parts of the **stone** <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or the starting material of the Great Work.</note>, approaching the nature of fire, are naturally able to be resolved more quickly by means of water so that they are afterward converted into air, it is expedient to separate those first by means of **Z** <note>The letter Z represents a specific degree of heat or a specific stage in Lull's symbolic alphabet system.</note> as a stony body; and thus earth with earth will give its humidity, and fire with fire, and each one by itself will provide its true "airy nature" in its rectifications.

The "Lion" usually represents the fixed or acidic matter (like gold or antimony) that the alchemist must "hunt" and "tame" to extract its essence.</note>, by the benefit and ingenuity of human discretion under the preservation of that property, [sepa]rating from the fire...

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

The **washing of water** is done by distilling it seven times by itself without any addition, time after time, with the **burial in dung** <note>original: *inhumatione fimi*. **of the living, food of the dead** <note>original: *viventium, cibus mortuorum*. From which it is clear, that our **quicksilver** is not [common] quicksilver...

However, do not think to draw out the work from that [substance] alone through any mastery: because by itself it will never arrive at the final stage of perfection. Whence that same **pagan** <note>original: *paganus*. In alchemical texts, this often refers to an ancient authority, likely an Arabic author like Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), whom Lull frequently cites.</note> and we similarly testify for certain by our manifest experiences, that it is perfected when mixed with a **body** <note>A solid metallic substance or "seed."</note> or with its earth, which is **sulfurous** <note>original: *sulphurea*. In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine principle that "fixes" the volatile mercury.</note>; and without that, the mastery is prolonged even unto despair.

And the reason for this is: that although Art strives to follow Nature as much as possible, yet in this it cannot imitate her entirely, except by a certain similarity. Art supplies what is missing in this step by leading the substance back to Nature, which Nature herself cannot do, since she does not possess such matter which, by its own property, must of necessity harden its **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver<note>Mercury. Here, it refers to the volatile alchemical principle that must be "indurated" or solidified to achieve perfection.</note></term>, so that Art may work in a shorter and briefer time than Nature.

For this reason we say that what Art accomplishes in a few days through multiple **digestions** <term>digestions<note>original: *digestionibus*. The application of gentle, continuous heat to mature a substance, analogous to how the stomach processes food.</note></term> upon the earth—without doing harm to Nature—heat accomplishes in minerals by a simple digestion in a thousand years. The method, indeed, of this **short...** <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword *bre-* (likely *breuitatis* or "shortness").</note>

<meta>catchword: short-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that alchemical 'Art' can accelerate natural processes, achieving in days through controlled heat and 'digestions' what nature takes a millennium to perform in the earth. It emphasizes that the 'quicksilver of the Philosophers' must be joined with a sulfurous 'body' to reach perfection.</summary>

**equality** <note>original: *aequalitatis*. He did not care to hide it as one greedy for the art, but rather as a leader in the **mastery** <term>mastery<note>original: *magisterio*. The specific laboratory procedures and theoretical knowledge required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term>, he left it to his successors for the future by revealing it through clear truth and by commending it faithfully and clearly. For he openly said: **Its nurse is the earth** <note>original: *Nutrix eius est terra*. A direct quote from the *Emerald Tablet of Hermes*, a foundational text of Hermeticism.</note>, since it is certain that the power of the **stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> is truly in that earth in its entirety; yet not fully, unless the **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver<note>original: *argentum vivum*. Mercury, representing the volatile, feminine principle that must be "fixed" by the sulfurous earth.</note></term> has been reduced into that same earth.

And for this reason, he adds that **when it ascends from the earth into heaven, and again descends into the earth, then it shall receive the power of the superiors and the inferiors** <note>Another quote from the *Emerald Tablet*, describing the process of sublimation and condensation which unites the "spiritual" and "earthly" qualities.</note>, namely its complete power, when it has been turned into its own earth. Others have called this earth the **Green Lion** <note>original: *Leonem viridem*. A common alchemical symbol often representing raw mineral matter or a powerful acidic solvent capable of "dissolving" gold.</note>, strong in battle. Others call it the **devouring Dragon** <note>original: *Draconem devorantem*.</note>, congealing or **killing its own tail** <note>A reference to the *Ouroboros* (the snake eating its tail), symbolizing the cyclical nature of the work and the "fixation" of volatile mercury.</note>, that is, its own quicksilver. Others have called it a **deserted place**; because it has been depopulated of its spirits. Others call it **poison**, because it is a killer. Others a **tree**, because it is fruit-bearing. Others call it the hidden **Hyle** <term>Hyle<note>From the Greek word for "matter." In alchemy, it refers to the primordial, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made.</note></term>, because it is the foundation of all nature, and

**subject of all elements.** <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page regarding the reduction of quicksilver.</note> Furthermore, do not forget, my son, this earth which we have named literally, while through many and diverse works it has flowed mostly through means and extremes: because the **Art** <note>Alchemy was often referred to simply as "The Art."</note> always needs **calx** <term>calx<note>original: *calce*. A powder or friable substance produced by roasting a metal or mineral; the "ashes" of a substance.</note></term>, that is, its own proper earth in which the mineral power is placed, especially for the purpose of **hardening Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium indurandum*. This refers to "fixing" the volatile liquid mercury into a solid state.</note>. For this reason, there is a need for **white lead** <note>original: *cerusa*. Traditionally lead carbonate, used here as a specific alchemical ingredient.</note>, **sulfur**, and **arsenic**, or for **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver<note>original: *argento vivo*. Mercury.</note></term> congealed into sulfur by the power of its own earth, for the purpose of hardening and coloring by art and nature. 

And therefore the same **pagan** <note>original: *paganus*. Likely referring to Hermes Trismegistus or another ancient non-Christian authority like Geber or Aristotle.</note> adds that with its companion in nature and its own substance—that is, **sulfur**, procreated through the power of its own earth—the **tincture** <term>tincture<note>original: *tinctura*. An elixir or substance capable of transmuting or "dyeing" base metals into gold or silver.</note></term> is made. It gives complete weight to every metal, and purifies and brightens it from foulness. This is evident through the preparations and reductions of that [sulfur] into its own body, for then it washes it of its foulness, whitens it, and perfects it through the **mastery** <term>mastery<note>original: *magisterio*. The specific laboratory procedures and theoretical knowledge required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term>, without which none of these things would excel. 

Therefore, my son, keep such **water** <note>In this context, "water" usually refers to a distilled mercurial liquid or a solvent.</note> distilled seven times, or such quicksilver, aside in a well-sealed glass vessel. Indeed, the sign of that aforementioned washing is a transparent and shining splendor, like the clarity of crystal; sometimes with dregs <note>The text cuts off here at "fecibus" (dregs/sediment).</note>

...white, in the manner of crystalline salt, which remains at the bottom after the seventh distillation, and sometimes without dregs: because then the water begins to settle, greatly desiring to be finished and to be congealed into **sulfur**, because it possesses little **phlegm** <term>phlegm<note>original: *phlegma*. In alchemical theory, this is the watery, tasteless, and inactive portion of a substance that remains after distillation.</note></term>. And when you see nothing remain at the bottom, it is a sign that the small amount of "body" <note>The solid, material aspect of the substance as opposed to its "spirit" or vapor.</note> has been raised up into much water: and therefore, because its pores could not be filled on account of the smallness of the body, it suffers from rarity <note>Meaning it is too thin or porous.</note>, desiring to attract rather than to release. And just as you did with the first water, so also shall you do with the second water, always storing the dregs with the aforementioned earth.

### On the extraction and increase of the air of both sexes, and the mortification of fire and earth.

#### CHAPTER XL.

Son, in your practical work and the extraction of **air** and **fire**, you will be able at one and the same time, in any turn and without interruption, to wash each separate **fire** and **earth** by themselves through **calcination** <term>calcination<note>original: *calcinatio*. The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in order to reduce it to a dry powder or ash, effectively "killing" its previous form.</note></term>, and to extract the **air** through **distillation**, until nothing of the **souls** <note>In alchemy, the "soul" is the volatile, spiritual essence of a substance that gives it its properties.</note> remains perceptibly within the **bodies** <note>The "body" refers to the solid, physical remains of the matter being worked upon.</note>. Therefore, first take the **air** and **fire** in the form of a thick liquid substance or viscous oil previously set aside: And from there, distill all the **air** through ashes <note>Distilling "through ashes" refers to using a sand or ash bath to provide a gentle, even heat to the vessel.</note>, exercising the greatest caution lest the fire ignite within the **tinctures** <term>tinctures<note>original: *tincturis*. The potent, concentrated essences capable of "dyeing" or transforming other base metals into gold or silver.</note></term>. And when the **air** has been distilled, the **fire** will remain at the bottom below, dry, tending toward blackness and redness: because there is a little blackness and much whiteness with a citron-yellow color. This blackness is in the white, and in the red, because it requires red and most pure **sulfur**, just as the white requires bright and white **sulfur**. And what you do in the operation for the white, the same you shall do in

## *On the washing of fire and earth through mortification.*

### CHAPTER XLI.

Son, the **washing** <note>original: *ablutio*. A purification process where the substance is cleansed of impurities through the application of liquid (alchemical "waters").</note> of **earth** is done with the first rectified water: and the washing of **fire** is done with the second: although it is not the greatest error if the earth is prepared in a similar way with the second water, provided that the water itself has first been well **rectified** <term>rectified<note>original: *rectificata*. Purified and strengthened through the process of repeated distillation.</note></term>, since it is hotter than the first: and for that reason, it is reduced into **air** more quickly. If, therefore, you wish to prepare the two said stony elements with the second water, take it and divide it into two parts. With one part, rectify the **earth**, and with the other part, rectify the **fire**—each separated together—mixing the water of earth with the earth, and the water of fire with the fire, in every preparation from then on. Truly, separate the waters through **distillation** in baths <note>original: *balneis*. Specifically referring to a *balneum Mariae* or water bath, which provides a gentle, indirect heat.</note>. From then on, set each substance aside, and do not mix—

[do not] mix <note>original: *misceas*. The text begins mid-word, continuing the command from the previous page.</note> the **white water** with the **red**, nor the red with the white: because with the white you will perform the **whitening** <note>original: *albificabis*. This refers to the *albedo* stage, or the creation of the Silver Stone.</note>, and with the red you will perform the **reddening** <note>original: *rubificabis*. This refers to the *rubedo* stage, the final perfection creating the Golden Stone.</note>. Nevertheless, do not think, my son, that this water appears red to the sight; rather, it appears white, though it carries within it the power of reddening with actual effect. 

And this is so because the nature of the venerable **fire** is hidden in the "belly of the air." It is not a most proximate subject that retains the **tincture** <term>tincture<note>original: *tincturam*. In alchemy, a substance capable of deeply penetrating and transforming the color and nature of a base metal.</note></term> in its outward appearance, and it cannot manifest it against the battle of fire until it has first been congealed into a well-complexioned **sulfur**. This is because when the water is sown in the smallest parts over the white **foliated earth** <term>foliated earth<note>original: *terram foliatam*. A purified, flaky, or layered state of the alchemical matter that has been cleansed of its "blackness."</note></term>, then the water recedes through drying by an expulsive virtue. Then, by a conjugal bond or the "love of nature," the **soul** remains bound. This soul is the tincture in the **Lion** <note>original: *Leone*. The "Lion" (often the Red Lion) is a common symbol for the fixed, active sulfur or the corrosive acidic water that "devours" the metals.</note>, in its own fixed body, which, according to its restraint, is capable of manifesting itself against the battle of fire. 

When, therefore, you have **distilled** the waters through waters, and allowed them to cool with diligence, place the vessels immediately among the ashes. Then perform the distillation of the **air** with the **fire of sawdust** <term>fire of sawdust<note>original: *igne de serratura*. A method of applying gentle, steady heat using burning sawdust or a slow-combustion furnace.</note></term>—white to white, and red to red. And when they have been distilled, keep them separately.

### *On the first roasting of fire and earth after the extraction of air.*

## CHAPTER 44.

Immediately, therefore, when you see the oil distilled from both bodies, continue the same fire for six hours without removing the receiving vessel, so that the bodies may be gently calcined <term>calcined<note>original: *calcinentur*. To reduce a substance to a fine powder or "ash" through prolonged heating.</note></term>, digested <term>digested<note>original: *digerantur*. A process of slow, constant heat that matures the substance, similar to metabolic digestion.</note></term>, and ultimately refined <note>original: *subtilientur*. To make a substance "subtle" or more spiritually pure by breaking down its physical density.</note>.

### *Roasting is nothing other than the preservation of bodies in the fire.*

## CHAPTER 45.

Furthermore, in doing this with careful management of the fire for the sake of perfection, do not allow yourself to have any doubt; for this roasting <note>original: *assatio*. Also translated as parching or scorching.</note> is nothing other than the preservation of those bodies in the fire for the period already stated by us.

### *On the second roasting of fire and earth.*

## CHAPTER 69.

When, however, in the practice already mentioned, the oil has been separated from the bodies—namely, earth and fire—by means of a dry fire of sawdust through distillation, apply a very gentle fire of coals, so that, like the sharp point of a subtle lance <note>A metaphor for focused, piercing heat.</note>, the bodies may be calcined by it for two or three hours. You must use the utmost caution to ensure that the vessel or the material does not become red-hot, lest the souls flee and the tinctures <margin>without the spirits.</margin> are burned. Because you have no necessary need for the red souls to flee from their bodies or their spirit due to excessive fire; otherwise, they will fail through <margin>Otherwise: without</margin> their departure, and will flee to a remote region along with the combustion of the tincture. And this is because the soul has no proper place by which it might be able to retain itself, dwell, or rest. Its restraint is the water with which the soul (which is fire) is extracted, little by little with a slow hunt <note>A traditional alchemical term for a patient, gradual extraction process.</note>, protecting it from burning...

And the soul itself is sulfur, and the forming power, sealing the species of things according to their kind, like masculine seed. And therefore, through the washing of water and the drying of fire, you always extract the oil from the hearts of the statues <note>original: *statuarum*. In alchemical allegory, "statues" or "images" often refer to the solid, "dead" metallic bodies before they are "enlivened" by the process.</note>; because the soul is fire in likeness, and a hidden fire, carried in the belly of the water, from which results air—which air is tinted water—and its tincture <note>The "tincture" is the concentrated essence or color-giving quality that performs the transformation.</note> is nothing but fire, <margin>It was not in another.</margin> nor is that fire itself anything but a body refined to the ultimate degree, and now hidden in the belly of the air. From which it is most clearly evident that air is the spirit carrying the fire, and the stone is fire carried in the belly of the air. If, therefore, you mix any quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury (*argentum vivum*), the liquid metal considered a primary building block of the Philosopher's Stone.</term> with such a fire, it will immediately and without separation be red forever. ...the **bodies** <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refers to the solid metallic or mineral materials being worked upon.</note> have been well burnt and reduced into a very fine powder. <margin>subtle</margin> And this is because in none of our operations do we need **water**, except for that which is clear and white, being close to the simple soul; nor do we need **oil** except for that which is white, pale yellow, or saffron-colored; nor **fire**, except for red; nor **earth** except for that which is pale and whitish, with a subtle and fixed substance. For the earth itself dries and fixes, while the water cleanses and washes. **Air** and **fire**, however, tint and cause the matter to flow. For this reason, the **Philosopher** <note>likely referring to Aristotle or a legendary figure like Hermes Trismegistus</note> teaches that the craftsman must necessarily have much water and much air; because however great the quantity of fire and water shall be, so great...

When, indeed, the humidity of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti viui*; another name for mercury.</note> has been thoroughly resolved from the bodies by the method described, distill it through an **alembic** <term>alembic: a distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for purifying liquids.</term> in a **Mary’s bath** <term>balneo Mariæ: a water bath (bain-marie) used to provide gentle, uniform heat.</term> [seven times], with a **burial** <note>original: *inhumatione*; refers to placing the vessel in a warm medium like sand, ash, or fermenting horse dung to maintain a steady, low temperature.</note> in the slowest heat always interspersed. For their **washing** <note>original: *ablutio*; the process of purifying a substance by removing impurities through liquid or heat.</note> is the same as it was for the first water: which indeed can be done by burying and distilling alike, until the air and oil reach a crystalline clarity without **dregs** <note>original: *fecibus*; the sediment or waste left after distillation.</note>. ### CHAPTER LIII.

Indeed, there are three **humidities** <note>original: *humiditates*; in alchemy, these are the liquid principles or "moistures" that hold the substance together</note>. The first is water, the head of those things that can be dissolved. The second is air, the middle between water and oil. The third humidity is the oil itself, the **softener** <term>ceratiuum: from "ceratio," the process of softening a hard substance to a wax-like consistency to prepare it for fusion or "projection."</term> of all the elements...
