...and our final and ultimate secret.

The first **humidity** <note>original: *humiditas*; in alchemy, these are the liquid or "moist" principles that hold a substance together</note> is separated by a **water bath** <term>water bath: also known as a bain-marie, used for gentle, indirect heating</term>, because it is hot. The second is separated in a dry fire of **iron filings** <term>iron filings: a method of dry heating where the vessel is surrounded by iron scales to reach higher temperatures than water</term>, because it is hotter. The third is separated in a dry fire of coals, because it is the hottest. And if the oil is cold, the air will be colder, and the water will be the coldest. And you should understand this by way of similarity regarding the **earths** <note>original: *terris*; the solid residues or base materials</note>, if you wish to effectively examine the natures of the **stone** <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note>: for the virtues of nature always take their names from their substances, and according to how their subjects undergo alterations, they also work and are changed in different ways; for through such alterations, bodies always pass from one nature into another. And the more their nature is subjected to the depths, the more it is gradually **sublimated** <term>sublimated: refined or "raised" from a solid state to a spiritual or vaporous state</term> toward super-celestial virtues; and the more it is raised to celestial virtues, the more noble and higher are its operations, and it is exalted, abounding in its own actions.

If you wish to know the reason, my son, why **our earth** <note>the true alchemical substance</note> is more effective at penetrating and congealing **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury</term> than the **earth of the sophists** <term>earth of the sophists: substances used by "sophists" or false alchemists who do not understand the true natural principles</term>, know that it is because the dryness of our earth, through the digestion of a subtle **complexional heat** <note>original: *caloris complexionalis*; the internal, inherent heat that governs the balance of a substance's qualities</note>...

### CHAPTER 54.

There are, therefore, three earths. The **first** is dissolved from metal, and that is simple, with which the first water is joined. This is because everything that is of a subtle and similar substance is generated more quickly and swiftly than what is thick and solid. This is because that which is of a subtle and rare substance is joined more quickly with what is subtle and rarefied, and is born more swiftly than that which is thick, hard, and compact. Therefore, a hard, compact, and heavy body requires many strengths of **digestion** <term>digestion: a slow, gentle heating process modeled on the way a stomach matures food</term> before it can become rare and light.

...therefore, every transmutable body, in order for it to be transformed into any species, must necessarily be reduced to its **first matter** <term>first matter: the formless, primal substance from which all things are made; in Latin, *materia prima*</term>, and first into **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; the alchemical name for mercury, considered the "mother" or "womb" of metals</note>. Then, it is reduced into sulfur through the second preparation already mentioned. 

In this matter, we have disagreed with **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle's *Meteorology*, Book IV, was a foundational text for medieval science and alchemy, though Lull here asserts his own experimental authority over the philosopher's theory</note> in the fourth book of his *Meteorology*, where he states that every passive body is reduced to its first matter through operations contrary to its nature. Likewise, it is necessary to cleanse soluble bodies by stripping away accidental "sulfur-ness" until they are as white as **crystal** and deprived of all greenness <note>In alchemical symbolism, "greenness" often refers to the raw, unrefined state of matter or "the green lion" which must be purified</note>. This is because if any sulfurous residue remains in the metallic bodies, it is impossible for them to be joined with the **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances like mercury or distilled essences that can evaporate or "fly"</term>.

->*Once the contrary is removed, its opposite must necessarily be introduced.*<-

### CHAPTER 57.

AND therefore bodies are **calcined** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a fine, dry powder</term> so that they may be dissolved more quickly. And these same bodies are dissolved so that they may be freed from oily filth and sulfurous...

-># LITTLE BOOK<-

...and from other impurities arising from insufficient **digestion** <term>digestion: a slow, steady heating process that mimics the natural maturation of minerals in the earth</term>, which prevent the bodies from proper **fixation** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to fire</term> and **fusion** <term>fusion: the melting or blending of substances together</term>, let them be better cleansed. This is for the same reason that when a contrary quality is removed, its opposite must necessarily be introduced.

Another reason is so that they may be more suited for penetration—namely, so they can be joined to the dissolving and **Mercurial moisture** <note>original: *Mercuriali humiditati*; the fluid, metallic essence that alchemists believed could dissolve and renew solid metals</note>. Thus, they are reduced to the state of **iron scales** or **burnt copper** <note>original: *squammæ ferri aut æris combusti*; the flaky byproduct of heating metal, used here as a metaphor for a purified, powdery state</note>, becoming like light and spongy bodies.

Because of this sponginess, a most subtle moisture later enters them, restoring what was lost and strengthening the bodies, so that by natural heat it may be gradually congealed into **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur*; here referring to the stable, active principle that gives the metal its final form</note> even in the smallest particles.

Hence, **the Pagan** <note>original: *paganus*; Lull likely refers to an Arabic alchemical authority, such as Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), whose works were highly respected by Western scholars but labeled "pagan" due to their origin</note> understood this most effectively when he says at the end of the chapter titled "What is **Sublimation**?" <term>sublimation: the purification of a substance by turning it into vapor and back to solid</term>: that the best method is to take the substance of burnt iron, **tin** <note>original: *stanni*</note>, or copper. Because they are deprived of their excessive moisture, they are easily coagulated—or **arsenic** <note>original: *arsenicum*</note> dissolved in silver...

For we do not only extract the **sulfur-ness** <note>original: *sulphureitatem*; the oily, impure quality alchemists believed caused metals to be combustible or imperfect</note> from the bodies, but also that moisture by which the **natural heat** <term>natural heat: the innate energy within matter that drives its growth and transformation</term> is dulled. This ensures that from all these substances, one single thing is created: stable, penetrating, **tinging** <term>tinging: the power of the alchemical medicine to instantly change the color and nature of another metal</term>, and permanent.

## *On the inquiry into why the second dissolution is performed, and what it is.*

### CHAPTER LVIII.

We perform the **second dissolution** <note>original: *dissolutionem...secundam*; the process of breaking down a substance into liquid form for a second time to further refine it</note> for two reasons. The first is so that we may obtain the spirits in a subtle state, derived from the very substance of the bodies, in the manner of clear water. The second reason is so that we may have bodies that are spongy and light by nature, deprived of their own [original] moisture, in which the **heat of nature** is active, penetrating and **congealing** <term>congealing: the process of turning a liquid substance, like mercury, into a solid state through alchemical action</term> its own **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum viuum*; mercury</note>, and converting it into **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur*; in this context, the active, "masculine" principle of the Philosopher's Stone, rather than common brimstone</note>. Because...

<summary>This page explains the purpose of the 'second dissolution,' which aims to strip away dulling moisture to create a 'spongy' body capable of transforming quicksilver into stable sulfur. The goal is to create a unified, 'tinging' substance that can permanently transform other metals.</summary>

-># THE CODICIL<-

He who does not coagulate the **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: historically known as *argentum vivum* (living silver) or mercury; in alchemy, it represents the principle of fluidity and volatility</term> extracted from the bodies by converting it into its own **white sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur proprium album*; this refers to the fixed, stable state of the matter after it has been purified and "whitened"</note>, has not chosen the correct path for the preparation.

If, therefore, you have been well-practiced in natural **sublimations** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as it cools back into a solid, used by alchemists to purify materials</term>, you will know that we have spoken the truth: that one must not omit the application of the **dregs** <note>original: *fæcum*; the solid residue or "earth" left behind after distillation or sublimation, which Lully argues is essential to the process</note> with which the substances to be sublimated must agree. You must unite them by the attraction of moisture in their very depths.

This is because it is necessary for them to consist of the proper substance of the spirits, and be arranged thinly in an **aerial substance** <note>original: *substantia aërea*; a gaseous or vaporous state</note>, so as to take back those same prepared spirits through their **smallest parts** <note>original: *per minima*; a medieval term referring to the fundamental, microscopic particles of a substance</note>. Nothing else can be found that can take their place in the **Mastery** <term>Mastery: often called the *Magisterium*, it refers to the entire alchemical process or the "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher’s Stone</term>. Since no spirit of the same kind is found that can mix with them through their smallest parts, they could not be joined by a natural union.

And if they were not mixed as is fitting, the entire substance of the spirits would ascend without any cleansing, and thus they would be found to be in no way purified after their ascension. Therefore, whoever labors to sublimate our quicksilver <note>alchemists often used "our" to distinguish their secret, philosophical materials from common commercial substances</note> using a foreign substance, strives in vain to sublimate it according to its nature. But just as you sublimate with the **calxes of the two luminaries** <term>two luminaries: the Sun (Gold) and the Moon (Silver); their "calxes" are the fine powders produced by heating the metals</term>, you will sublimate usefully, and you will cleanse...

Lully warns that attempting to purify "philosophical mercury" with foreign substances is futile, recommending instead the use of gold and silver powders.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Lully’s technical instructions on the "Mastery" or Great Work, moving from the physical preparation of spirits to the philosophical principle of "like produces like." -># RAYMOND LULLY<-

...with ease, because their **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance into vapor and then cooling it back into a solid to purify it</term> is easy. That which is not foreign to it—into which **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: historically known as *argentum vivum* or mercury; the fluid, volatile principle in alchemy</term> is converted through our **Mastery** <term>Mastery: the *Magisterium*, or the entire alchemical process of transformation</term>—is namely **white and red sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur album & rubeum*; these are the "fixed" or stable principles of matter. In alchemy, the "white" stage leads to silver, and the "red" stage leads to gold</note>, from which silver and gold are created. 

For if we possessed sulfur and mercury made from that same material above the earth, from which gold and silver are naturally formed beneath the earth, we could easily make gold and silver from them according to the properties of their own nature. Therefore, nothing is suitable for a thing unless it is closely related to it by its own nature. 

For you see that a human is not generated except from a human, nor are other **perfect animals** <note>original: *perfectis animalibus*; a medieval biological classification for complex animals that reproduce their own likeness</note> born except from their own kind. This is because every individual thing is generated so that it follows that which is similar to itself through the process of generation. 

Therefore, we use a **venerable matter** <note>original: *venerabili materia*; the sacred or fundamental base substance of the Great Work</note>, since it cannot be improved except by reducing it into its own original matter. Into this, you must not introduce anything foreign—no powder, no water, nor any other thing—except its own proper quicksilver in its most closely related form.

<summary>Lully explains that the creation of gold and silver requires "white and red sulfur" derived from mercury, arguing that alchemical transformation must follow the natural law where like produces like.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Raymond Lully’s technical instructions from the "Codicillus" (The Small Codicil). <page-num>110</page-num>
# CODICIL

->*The earth must be composed of two bodies, namely*<-
->*the Sun and the Moon, and two elements are dry*<-
->*and stony, namely fire and earth.*<-

### CHAPTER LIX.

Therefore, with the bodily remnants most fitting in nature—namely those of the **Sun and Moon** <note>original: *Solis & Lunæ*; in alchemical shorthand, these always represent gold and silver</note>—it is necessary to mix **Mercury** <term>Mercury: often referred to as "the Soul" of the Work, it is the volatile, liquid principle that must be "fixed" into a solid</term> as minutely as possible, and to cook it down until it becomes **sulfur** <term>sulfur: the active, burning principle of stability and color in the Great Work</term>. You must then return the water back upon its own earth, so that it may recover the moisture it has lost. 

Generally speaking, that earth contains within itself a thin, subtle, and imperceptible dry white smoke which, by its very nature and substance, **congeals quicksilver** <note>original: *congelantem argentum viuum*; the process of turning liquid mercury into a solid, stable metal</note>. But since the earth must be composed of two bodies—gold and silver—and two of their elements are dry, hard, and stony (namely **fire and earth**, which agree in their quality of dryness), they must be prepared together. Because their qualities are compatible, they should undergo one and the same preparation, so that their smoky vapors may again be uniformly released during the third **dissolution** <term>dissolution: the process of liquefying or breaking down a substance into its fundamental parts</term> and be carried within the "belly" of Mercury.

<summary>Lully describes the preparation of the "Earth" of the Philosopher's Stone by combining gold and silver, then "fixing" mercury through the application of heat and the recycling of moisture.</summary>
<keywords>Sun and Moon, gold and silver, sulfur, mercury, quicksilver, four elements, fire and earth, dissolution, congealing</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues Lully’s technical instructions from the "Codicillus." Having established that the "Earth" of the work is a combination of the dry principles of gold and silver, he now explains how these elements interact with quicksilver to produce the "Quintessence" and the final red transformation.</meta>

<page-num>III</page-num>
# RAYMOND LULLY

Since the entire **fifth nature** <note>original: *natura quinta*; another term for the Quintessence, the pure celestial essence of a substance</note> of both bodies is congealed and retained: because when **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury, the volatile liquid metal representing the spirit</term> is constricted in the whitest earth by the power of the vapors of both aforementioned bodies, the **quintessence** is offered there, which had been infused by nature into the earths of those two bodies. And therefore, when quicksilver is clothed in radiant, two-part garments, the **foliated earth** <term>foliated earth: *terra foliata*; a purified, flaky state of the alchemical matter appearing during the whitening phase</term> will be closer to being dyed into **red sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur rubeum*; the final "red" stage of the Great Work, used for the transmutation of gold</note> than if it had been clothed simply in the white alone. This is especially true since, by its similar nature, it is now absorbed through such a binding into the smallest parts; and most of all because the quicksilver, containing the seed dissolved into spirit, is assimilated to its own sought-after object. It is necessary that it be drawn into the place where the **male seed** is cast, in which there is the heat of nature, forming and sealing: because in this way it will be turned toward the nature of the venerable temperament of the very place of generation, when the **union** <note>original: *coitus*; Lully uses biological metaphors to describe the chemical bonding of active (male) and passive (female) principles</note> of the matter of both sexes was made—namely, of the earths of the **Sun and Moon** <note>original: *Solis & Lunæ*; gold and silver</note>—and of all those things which surround such a generation. Join, therefore, the thicker substance of the Sun with the earth of the Moon after the extraction of the air, and prepare...

<summary>Lully describes how quicksilver (mercury) acts as a medium to extract the Quintessence from the "earths" of gold and silver. He uses the metaphor of sexual generation to explain how these combined elements form the "red sulfur" necessary for the final transmutation.</summary>

### *How calcined and totally*
### *discontinued bodies should be prepared.*

## CHAPTER 60.

The preparation of those bodies which have been **calcined** <term>calcined: the process of heating a substance—usually a metal—to high temperatures until it is reduced to a dry, crumbly powder called a "calx"</term> is nothing other than for them to naturally recover a greater **humidity** <note>original: *humiditas*; in alchemy, this is the "radical moisture" or life-force that gives a substance its flexibility and vitality</note> than they had lost in the state of lime. Because when bodies are calcined, they are naturally deprived of that watery moisture which thickens and binds them together. Therefore, since they are dry and empty and naturally very thirsty—desiring to be strengthened by watery moisture—they congeal, by their own heat, that moisture which is found in the substance of **sulfur** <term>sulfur: here referring to the "Philosophical Sulfur," the active, fiery, and "male" principle of the alchemical work</term>. This is achieved through a direct operation upon the smallest parts, infusing the **male virtue** <note>original: *virtutem masculinam*; the active, formative power that acts upon the "female" or passive matter to create a new form</note> into it. Because since such bodies are the active principles for the sake of forming sulfur, they possess natural heat... <note>original: *possident*; the text ends with the fragment "possi-" which is completed by the catchword "dent"</note>

<summary>Lully explains that the purpose of preparing calcined metals is to restore the "radical moisture" lost during heating. He describes the dry, "thirsty" powder absorbing the "male virtue" of sulfur to begin the process of transformation.</summary>

For this reason, we do not intend to summarize those things here, except in so much as it might be useful for the immediate operation.

## *What the matter of the stone is, and what things are required*
## *for its creation.*

### C H A P T E R  L X I.

Indeed, the matter of our stone, or of the **sulfur** <term>sulfur: in alchemy, this represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle, often associated with the "soul" or internal heat of a metal</term>, is a seminal fluid separated through the act of generation from the individual parts of both parents—namely, the **Body** <note>original: *corporis*; referring here to the fixed metallic substance, usually gold or the "father" of the stone</note> and **Mercury** <term>Mercury: the passive, feminine, and fluid principle; it provides the "moisture" necessary for growth and serves as the "mother" in this metaphor</term>. Therefore, this matter, once dispersed in the place of conception by the attractive force of nature, is gathered within the cells—that is, the earthy pores of the **womb** <note>original: *matricis*; the alchemical vessel or the "earthy" environment where the chemical "conception" occurs</note>—and is mixed together through heat along with a certain grinding.

And unless there occurred a mixing of the moisture—now made raw again—from both parents (namely, the Body and Mercury), the creation of our child, or our sulfur, could not take place. Furthermore, the matter of **love** <note>original: *amoris*; Lully frequently uses the language of human attraction and reproduction to describe chemical affinity and the bonding of substances</note>, overflowing with moisture or vapor proceeding from the body, is exceedingly dry and hot. For this reason, because of its excessive dryness, it would be unable to expand itself uniformly or maintain its continuity without the loss of its own substance. Moreover, the matter would be burned up by the superabundance of the sulfur's heat during the **decoction** <term>decoction: the process of "cooking" or heating a substance in a liquid over a long period to mature or refine it</term>, and the substance of the sulfur would be destroyed, were it not for the moisture of Mercury (which possesses contrary qualities) providing a moisten-

## CHAPTER LXII.

Just as **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: Latin *argentum vivum*; mercury, seen here as the feminine, receptive principle</term>, by its power and property, attracts the conquered **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur*; the masculine, active principle</note> to itself through the conversion of that sulfur into quicksilver: so sulfur, by a contrary operation and its own property, can conquer the quicksilver and convert it into itself through its **reduction** <term>reduction: the process of returning a substance to a previous or more "fundamental" state, in this case, solidifying the liquid mercury into the "sulfur of nature"</term>. The nature of **digestion** <term>digestion: Latin *digestio*; in alchemy, this refers to a process of slow, gentle heating that matures or "cooks" a substance over time, similar to how the stomach processes food</term> is when **natural heat** <term>natural heat: Latin *calor naturalis*; the innate warmth believed to drive all biological and chemical transformations</term> completely overcomes its subject, or at least does so completely as it is contained in all nutrients. Therefore, it is clear that a **digestive medicine** <note>original: *medicina digestiva*; a substance used to facilitate the maturation of the work</note>, in the absolute sense of the name, is that which possesses a heat conforming in quantity and quality to natural heat, through which it strengthens that same heat to complete the **transmutation** <note>original: *transmutationem*; the changing of one substance into another</note> of the food. This is most especially true in this **first digestion** <note>The initial stage of the alchemical process</note>; and this medicine is called "digestive" in an absolute sense because, in this stage, the digestive power encounters great resistance. 

And from these things you will be able to know whether the **earths of the bodies** <note>original: *terrae corporum*; the solid, material remains of the substances being worked upon</note> ought to be joined to one another, since one is strengthened by the other. Furthermore, you should know that **our quicksilver** <term>our quicksilver: Latin *argentum vivum nostrum*; "Philosophical Mercury," a purified or spiritualized version of mercury distinct from the common liquid metal</term> is composed of a different **complexion** <term>complexion: the specific balance of the four qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) that determines a substance's nature</term> and is characterized by a different nature than the **common quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum vulgi*; ordinary mercury found in nature or trade</note>, the nature of which is most distant from our **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur*; the active, masculine principle of the Stone</note>. 

And conversely, between sulfur and our quicksilver there is no difference because they naturally agree in their type of complexion, even though one is by its nature moist and the other dry. We speak more extensively of the manner of this **maturation** <note>original: *maturationis*; the process of ripening the Stone</note> below in our **Testament** <note>Lully refers to his famous work, the *Testamentum*, a cornerstone of the alchemical corpus</note>, where we have treated the **animation of Mercury** <term>animation of Mercury: the process of "bringing to life" or activating the philosophical mercury so it can interact with other elements</term> in full. # CODICILLVS

Since this process arises from the body’s own substance, it is necessary that the substance agrees with that body in **complexion** <term>complexion: Latin *complexio*; the specific balance of the four primary qualities (hot, cold, moist, and dry) that determines a substance's nature</term>, essence, quality, and nature. And because the transition into being is easier for those substances that share a **common symbol** <note>original: *habentibus symbolum*; an Aristotelian concept where elements with shared qualities—such as both being "moist"—transform into one another more readily</note>, the swift generation of the **sulfur** and the **stone** will be available to you. Since, therefore, the sulfurous body cannot ascend upward like a spirit unless the spirit has first been incorporated with it **through its smallest parts** <term>through its smallest parts: Latin *per minima*; a term referring to the most minute particles or "atoms" of a substance, suggesting a total molecular union rather than a simple mixture</term>.

Therefore, pour in the **first water** <note>This refers to the initial solvent or "mercurial water" used in the work</note> gradually and by turns—that is, time after time—by grinding and cooking it through the smallest parts until it is congealed. The fire, however, must be applied in such a regulated way that the **forming and sealing power** <note>original: *virtus formans & sigillans*; the innate intelligence or force within matter that gives it its specific shape and properties</note> which exists in the matter may proceed from the natural heat. 

When, however, the water has been congealed, the dry matter is **calcinated** <term>calcinated: Latin *calcinat*; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter or reduce it to a fine powder</term> by lightly continuing the fire, producing a moderate dryness to complete the following digestion. An **alembic** <term>alembic: Latin *alembicum*; a classic alchemical distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</term> should be placed on top so that, during the transformation of the digestion, the wateriness of the **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti vivi*; common mercury</note> itself may be separated and rise up unharmed. 

Thus, through the **circulation** <term>circulation: Latin *circulatione*; a process where a fluid is distilled and then allowed to flow back onto the solid remains in a continuous cycle</term>—of which the medicine is the "daughter"—it is artificially brought about that the sulfur is digested by the ingenuity of nature. It is then fully prepared for the subsequent **decoction** <term>decoction: Latin *decoctionem*; a process of boiling or heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or mature it</term>, passing thereafter through the organs of Mercu- <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing "Mercury" on the following page.</note>

...mercuries <note>original: *mercurii*; the text starts mid-word, completing "mercuries" from the previous page</note>, through the boiling of which it is artificially made raw and poured into the cavities of the bodies, and finally, having been altered by the power of natural hot **sulfur**, it is constricted and returns into itself. For if the powers of the sulfur are overcome by the addition and excessive soaking of the water, the sulfur is weakened and dissolved...

<meta>This page follows the discussion on the necessity of uniting sulfur and mercury "down to the smallest particles" (per minima) to avoid losing the active spirit in a "sea of disturbance." # RAYMOND LULL

## On the homogeneity of metals.

### CHAPTER LXIII.

The primary **homogeneity** <term>homogeneity: the state of having a common nature; in alchemy, this refers to the shared "root" substance from which all metals are born</term> of metals is the female seed of metals—or the metallic seed—namely our **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: also known as mercury; the "mother" or passive principle in alchemy, representing fluidity and spirit</term> for the work of **reduction** <note>original: *reductionis*; the process of "leading back" a solid metal into its original, liquid, or "raw" state</note>. However, by its proximate nature, it is fit and congealed into **sulfur** <term>sulfur: the "father" or active principle, representing heat and the power to give solid form</term> for the purpose of **fixation** <note>original: *fixationem*; the process of making a substance stable and resistant to fire so that it no longer evaporates</note>.

This is because the **dissolution** <note>original: *solutio*; melting or breaking down</note> of the body into spirit occurs simultaneously with the congealing of the spirit into sulfur; and the congealing of the spirit into sulfur occurs simultaneously with the dissolution of the body into the spirit—which spirit is "congealed water." Therefore, the dissolved bodies are reduced to the nature of the spirit. They do not differ except in this: they are more "fixed" by reason of being bodies than the volatile spirit is, and by reason of the spirits, they are more "volatile" than the fixed bodies are.

For by the **love of nature** <note>original: *Amore naturae*; a common alchemical belief that substances are drawn together by natural sympathy or "attraction" similar to human love</note>, each of them acts upon a partner similar to itself. And therefore, he who wishes to **transmute** <note>original: *transmutare*; to change one species of metal into another, such as lead into gold</note> the species of metals will labor in vain unless he first reduces the bodies into such a matter. Only then can they be reduced into something other than what they were before. This is because once the first form of **Mercury’s** corruption is destroyed (as will be discussed more extensively below), another form is immediately introduced through transmutation.

<summary>This chapter explains that all metals share a "homogenous" root—mercury—and that to change a metal, an alchemist must first break it down into this shared "female seed" before reforming it with sulfur.</summary>

# THE CODICIL

...of **mercury** into **sulfur**. For a wise and diligent seeker can usefully **corrupt** <note>original: *corrumpere*; in an alchemical context, this refers to "breaking down" or "destroying" the current outward form of a metal so that it can be rebuilt into something new</note> the bodies and introduce a new form of sulfur into them. This is because the goal of our operation is nothing other than choosing the purest substance of Mercury to resolve <note>original: *resoluenda*; to break down or loosen</note> the bodies into sulfur, for the **Elixir** <term>Elixir: the perfected substance, often called the Philosopher's Stone, which has the power to transmute metals and perfect nature</term> consists of these alone.

Once the form of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti vivi*; literally "living silver," common mercury</note> has been transmuted into a sulfurous stone, another form is soon introduced through the conversion of that stone into the Elixir. This happens through a **temperament** <term>temperament: a balanced mixture or "tuning" of the primary qualities, specifically the "moist" and the "dry"</term> of the moist and the dry, just as we shall explain in the following section on **fixation** <note>original: *infixatione*; the process of making a substance stable and resistant to fire</note>.

From this, it is evident that there can be no transition from one **extreme** to another **extreme** except through a **medium** <note>original: *medium*; a middle state, bridge, or intermediary substance</note>. In this art, we take as the "medium" the spirit of the middle conjunction between two qualities. These qualities differ from each other in their specific appearance <note>original: *specie*</note>, but not in their essential nature <note>original: *genere*</note>, except by the greater or lesser distance between them. This distance is bridged during the **decoctions** <term>decoction: the alchemical process of "cooking" or heating a substance to mature it, mimicking natural geothermal processes</term> performed both by art <note>human skill and laboratory work</note> and by nature. 

Therefore, the varieties of this medium have a lesser similarity to the "temperament"—the exact point equidistant between extremes—and to the power <note>original: *virtute*</note> that resides in the center of those extremes. If your intellect has knowledge of this **medium**...

<summary>Lull explains that creating the Elixir requires breaking down metals into a "sulfurous stone" through a specific balance of moisture and dryness. He emphasizes the philosophical principle that change from one state to another must pass through a middle "medium" or bridge.</summary>

...in the aforementioned species, you will be able to preserve and restore health to all sick bodies <note>original: *corporibus infirmis*; in alchemy, "sick bodies" refers to base metals like lead or iron that have not yet been "perfected" into gold</note> and completely kill off their infirmity, provided you know how to bring the **medium** <term>medium: an intermediate substance or state that bridges two opposites, such as the solid and the volatile</term> from potential into act. From this, you can increase or decrease the power of this medium if you know how to join and separate the **flowers and leaves of the tree** <note>original: *flores arboris & frondes*; a common alchemical metaphor where the "flowers" represent volatile spirits and "leaves" represent the fixed physical matter</note> through **dissolution** and **congealing**, as we have handed down in our *Testament* <note>Lull's *Testamentum* was one of the most influential alchemical manuals of the Middle Ages</note>, concerning the multiplication of virtues while maintaining the proper conditions.

Therefore, to possess and lead such a medium from potential into act, join the **body** with the **spirit** in the manner that nature requires. This is done by gradually soaking it with the **Water of Mercury** <term>Water of Mercury: the "mercurial" solvent used to break down metallic bodies into their primary essence</term>, grinding it with fire alone through a slow boiling <note>original: *decoctione*; a process of heating a substance in liquid</note> so that they are mixed together by the drying of one and the dissolving of the other, until the whole becomes one.

In this way, by virtue of its simplicity, it may rise through **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collects again as a solid, used here to "spiritualize" or refine the matter</term> as if into a very thin and aerial earthy substance, which is part of the **permanent water** <note>original: *aquæ permanentis*; the "Philosophical Mercury," a stable, transformative solvent that remains effective even when subjected to heat</note>.

However, this is impossible unless the body—through the natural benefit of **decoction** <term>decoction: a slow, steady heating or "cooking" of the substance in a vessel</term>—is first incorporated with the spirit and the whole becomes one. Then, indeed, it rises upward "alive," depending on the greater or lesser decoctions and **imbibitions** <term>imbibition: the stage where the solid matter "drinks" or absorbs the liquid solvent until it is fully saturated</term> it underwent during its **dissolution**. Through the attraction of nature, that which is not fixed ascends and is **sublimated** as a **spirit**.

For this reason, we say that whatever ascends from the **body** <note>original: *corpus*</note> has been dissolved into a spirit. This spirit is like a point in the middle of two things, harmonizing perfectly with the **temperament** <note>the ideal balance of qualities</note>. Therefore, it is called "one body and one spirit," a single medium that retains within itself the nature of the extremes—namely, of **water and fire**—by virtue of being a natural middle ground between them, just as **air** sits between the heavens and the earth.

Thus, it is clear to the craftsman that the **dissolution** <term>dissolution: the process of turning a solid into a liquid or vaporous state</term> of the body occurs simultaneously with the **congealing** <note>original: *congelatio*</note> of the spirit; this is evident during the ascent of sublimation. The more immediate cause of this fixation is the mixing of both substances **through their smallest parts** <note>original: *per minima*; an early concept of mixing at the molecular or atomic level</note>, just as we have stated above. This must be done, however, in such a way that the total amount of the volatile does not exceed...

When it has been digested and congealed, strengthen it through its own power by separating the superfluous "urine" of the natural moisture which has already been converted by the natural roasting. 

This is done so that the power of the **sulfur** <note>the active, masculine, and "combustible" principle in alchemy</note> can, by its own sharpness and notable form, more readily congeal the remaining moisture of the spirit and retain that solidified matter by its own nature. You must repeat all these operations without becoming weary, time after time, through the **incorporation of Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurii incorporamentum*; the process of uniting the volatile mercurial spirit with the solid body</note>, until the **earth** <gloss>the solid base</gloss> has drunk as much of its **water** <gloss>the liquid solvent</gloss> as is required, which will be revealed to you by the sign mentioned previously.

### *On the calcination of the final soaking.*

## CHAPTER 61.

When, however, you have reached the end of the **cooking** <term>cooking: <note>original: *decoctionis*</note> *decoctio* is the prolonged heating of the substance in a sealed vessel to mature it</term> and the final **soaking** <term>soaking: <note>original: *imbibitionis*</note> the process of adding liquid to the dry "body" to nourish it</term>, **calcine** <term>calcine: <note>original: *calcina*</note> to heat the substance until it is reduced to a dry powder or "ash," driving off excess moisture</term> the body by expelling the "urinal" moisture <note>a term for the volatile, watery excess that must be removed</note>, as much as its dissolution will cast off gently and not hastily, lest the body be dissolved into smoke and the **Mastery** <term>Mastery: <note>original: *magisterium*</note> the "Great Work" or the completed Philosopher's Stone</term> be lost. 

Indeed, do not be weary of repeating this many times, although it be long, because you will not see a profitable **tincture** <term>tincture: <note>original: *tincturam*</note> the transformative power or "color" that allows the Stone to change base metals into silver or gold</term> until you complete the color of permanent whiteness, which is the second principal demonstrative stage. This is the color which is not burned by fire—as we have mentioned below—neither within nor without, but through greater cooking...

...fire, when it is transformed into **yellowness** <term>yellowness: <note>original: *citrinitatem*</note> the "Citrinitas," a transitional stage of the Work between the white and red, once considered a major phase</term>, which is the third main demonstrative principle; and finally, through succession and the digestion of a greater fire, it is concluded in **redness** <term>redness: <note>original: *rubedinem*</note> the "Rubedo," the fourth and final stage of the Great Work, signifying the completion of the Philosopher's Stone</term>, which is the sign of the completed number and the fourth demonstrative principle, by which the student must guide himself in the **Mastery** <term>Mastery: <note>original: *magisterium*</note> the successful completion of the alchemical process</term>. 

Therefore, let the craftsman study and prudently pay attention when he is engaged in the work to all the demonstrative signs that appear in every **decoction** <term>decoction: <note>original: *decoctione*</note> a process of prolonged heating or "cooking" the substance in a sealed vessel</term>; let him store those signs in his mind and wisely investigate their causes. For as long as the **earth** <note>the solid matter remaining in the vessel</note> emerges **black** <term>black: <note>original: *nigra*</note> the "Nigredo" or "black of blacks," the initial stage of putrefaction and dissolution</term>, it is imperfect and therefore not yet complete. 

Therefore, turn after turn, gradually strengthen the fire in the **calcination** <term>calcination: <note>original: *calcinatione*</note> the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter and reduce it to a powder</term> until, by the strength of the fire, the earth emerges **white** <term>white: <note>original: *alba*</note> the "Albedo," the stage of purification where the matter is compared to a white swan or shimmering silver</term>. For just as heat acting upon the moist first generates blackness, so heat acting upon the dry generates whiteness; and successively, it generates yellowness in that white substance. However, it is perfected with the addition of the **tinging substance** <note>original: *rei tingentis*—the agent that provides the final, permanent color and power to the Stone</note>, which is known to nature. 

This is seen in the calcination of **lead** <note>original: *plumbi*</note>, which is first turned into black ash, then into white, then into yellow, and afterwards into red. In this same way, **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur*—in alchemy, this often refers to the active, masculine principle of the Work rather than common brimstone</note> shall be obtained in both white and red.

...from the matter of metals fully purified, yet in a different way as has been declared, **cooked** <term>cooked: <note>original: *decocta*</note> the process of slow heating in a sealed vessel</term> and **digested** <term>digested: <note>original: *digesta*</note> a process where substances are softened and matured through gentle heat, mimicking biological digestion</term>. This action, although it is suited to coldness, is not suited to an <margin>Otherwise whiteness will be generated.</margin> **excessive cold** <note>original: *excellenti [frigiditati]*</note>, but rather to that which is constitutional and tempered, which retains the medicinal assistance of nature, so that the colors and the spirits fixed within it do not flow away from it.

And therefore, when the quantity of the congealed and refined moisture shall obtain the quantity of the body, whiteness is generated in the matter more than any other...

## CODICILLUS

...color, by reason of its own purified moisture: for in the moist there is always the nature of transparency. And this is the proper matter of color. And for this reason, the more our **earth** <note>original: *terra nostra*; the solid material or "salt" of the work</note> is washed with such moisture, the more it is whitened <note>original: *albificatur*; referring to the transition to the Albedo, or white stage of the Great Work</note>: and the more such moisture is purified, refined, and prepared, the more it is ennobled with a clearer, more subtle, and more serene color. 

Similarly, by the opposite logic, the less our nutritional moisture is prepared, and the further it is removed from the stage of **sublimation** <term>sublimation: <note>original: *sublimatio*</note> the process of heating a substance so it rises as vapor and collects again in purified form, spiritualizing the material</term>, the more it corresponds to **blackness** <note>original: *nigredini*; the dark, initial stage of decay or "death" of the matter known as Nigredo</note> and combustion <note>original: *combustioni*; charring or burning due to excessive heat</note>. And the closer it comes to combustion—provided that heat holds dominion within it—the more there will be smoke, darkness, and blackening. 

Therefore, most dear son, examine these things and the others I speak of: and prepare the elements so that they are clean from impurities and safe from combustion, until their **tincture** <note>original: *tinctura*; the concentrated essence or "soul" of the matter that has the power to transform and color other substances</note> is extracted. For if they are burned, they are stripped of their effect and utility. From these things you may gather that the preparation of the elements must be performed by art and by their conjunction, so that their tincture may be improved and preserved from all corruption.

<summary>This page explains that the "whiteness" of the alchemical work is achieved by washing the "earth" with purified moisture, while excessive heat leads to "blackness" and the destruction of the substance's medicinal potential. It warns the practitioner to protect the elements from "combustion" to ensure the essential "tincture" is preserved.</summary>

## *On the virtues and the doctrine by which error is corrected through the proper instruments.*

### CHAPTER LXVIII.

We say, moreover, that **virtue** <note>original: *virtus*; in this context, it refers to an inherent power, capacity, or active force within a substance that enables it to act or change</note> is the primary and particular principle of operation within its own matter. We used the word "primary" to distinguish it from our **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: <note>original: *argenti vivi nostri*</note> also known as mercury; for Lull, this is "philosophical mercury," the refined, fluid principle of matter that acts as the immediate agent in the work</term>, which is the proximate and immediate principle of the operation, acting as a dissolving or congealing agent. 

We used the word "particular" to distinguish it from the "conjoined body," which is the universal principle of the operation of **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphuris*; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and solar principle that provides heat, color, and "fixation" to the mercury</note> acting as a congealing or dissolving agent. Therefore, we do not intend for the types of virtues to be distinct from the essential substance itself, but only to be distinguished by their immediate operations and the origin they possess within the matter. 

Regarding the consideration of these immediate operations, there is one genus of virtues, namely the **natural** virtues. That is called "natural" which, through its operation, ensures that nature is principally preserved, and through which both the species and the individual are perfected. And of this genus, there are two types: for there are some which are served, and these are called the **administrative** <note>original: *ministrativæ*; these are the higher-level powers that direct or receive the effects of the "serving" powers</note> virtues. There are others, indeed, which are the serving natural virtues, which...

<summary>Lull defines "virtue" as the internal driving force of alchemical change and distinguishes between the roles of "philosophical" mercury and sulfur. He begins a taxonomic breakdown of these powers into "natural," "administrative," and "serving" virtues.</summary>

# CODICILLUS

...the generative, augmentative <note>original: *augmentatiua*; the power of growth or increasing in size</note>, and nutritive <note>original: *nutritiua*; the power of sustaining through nourishment</note> virtues are provided. Indeed, the generative virtue is that which works toward this end: that a similar body is procreated. This happens in two ways: either through a long and successive operation, which occurs by the smallest parts <note>original: *per minima*; a reference to the medieval theory that change happens at the level of the smallest possible particles of matter</note>; or through a prompt and brief sudden transformation, which occurs without sensibly or visibly passing through its intermediate stages.

And there are two works of this generative virtue. The first work of the generative virtue is in the womb or earth which is called the **vessel of Philosophy** <note>original: *vas Philosophiae*; this refers not just to a glass flask, but to the "philosophical earth" or environment where the "seed" of the stone is planted and transformed</note>, in which the power of nature is diffused. One is the "principle of passion" <note>original: *principium passionis*; "passion" here means the capacity to be acted upon or to undergo change</note> in the substance, by reason of which many and diverse instruments proceed from the matter, such as colors, accidents <note>original: *accidentia*; in Aristotelian philosophy, these are non-essential qualities like color or smell that can change without changing the substance itself</note>, and the like. But the other distinction is called "active," which acts...

Therefore, by the primary institution of nature, it is established that food must first be perfectly broken down by the teeth designated for this purpose before it is sent to the place of digestion. Thus, because this constriction and dilation are carried out by the **animal virtue** <note>original: *animali virtute*; in medieval science, the "animal" power refers to the "soul" (anima) that governs movement and sensation</note>, the ancients—though perhaps improperly—compared the process to it.

### CHAPTER LXIX.

Another work of the generative power is to provide form through the **informing virtue** <note>original: *virtute informante*; the power that gives specific shape, identity, and characteristics to raw matter</note>. This power is twofold. One is **intrinsic**, being naturally innate within the matter through nature; it is by this power that the matter of a metal is immediately transmuted. The other is **extrinsic**, and this depends upon the artisan <note>original: *artifice*; the alchemist performing the work</note>, insofar as he prepares and disposes the matter through his craft...

# THE CODICILLUS

...of nature, according to the requirement of the **informing power** <note>original: *virtutis informatiuæ*; the internal force that gives a substance its specific "form" or identity</note> which is within the matter. Because of this, we say that the **Alchemist** <term>Alchemist: <note>original: *Alchymiſta*</note> the practitioner of the Hermetic art</term> is nothing more than a simple administrator of nature. For he himself does not transmute the metal—nature does. He merely prepares the matter through his craft, and thus nature herself, who is keen and wise, completes the work of art. 

It is enough, therefore, for the artisan to wisely arrange and "inform" <note>original: *informare*; to give shape or character to</note> the matter from the outside according to the powers of nature, since she herself works sufficiently on her own from the inside toward her own perfection. Indeed, the movements belonging to it follow one path and one order in the best and most certain way that can be imagined, as is evident in the **inceration** <term>inceration: <note>original: *inceratione*</note> the process of adding liquid to a dry substance to soften it to a wax-like consistency</term> of any generated thing. 

Because of this, it is clear that the preparation in the **Mastery of the Philosophers** <note>original: *magiſterio Philoſophorum*; the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone</note> ought to be slow and gradual, so that nature cannot bypass the necessary stages of movement. The preparation is conducted for the sake of this "informing," whereby the matter receives influence from the artisan on the outside so that nature may complete its internal movements. 

Wherefore it is appropriate that the **power of the exciting heat** <note>original: *virtus caloris excitantis*; the external fire or heat applied to the vessel</note> does not exceed the power of the natural heat that has been awakened; otherwise, the movement will not be completed, but will pass into other states that do not belong to the power...

<summary>This page defines the alchemist as a mere "administrator of nature," arguing that the artisan only prepares the material while nature performs the actual transmutation. It emphasizes the need for a slow, gentle external heat to avoid disrupting the internal "natural heat" and the delicate process of informing the matter.</summary>

...-al <note>The text continues from the previous page's word *extra-*, forming *extrinsecae*, meaning "external."</note> external formation, which proceed immediately from practical knowledge, are as many as we have already said and are about to say, although a practiced person, if he is very intelligent, can greatly shorten or even lengthen them. 

And as for that first **blackness** <term>blackness: <note>original: *nigredine*</note>; the *Nigredo* is the first stage of the Great Work, representing the "death" or "putrefaction" of the matter, where it is reduced to its primal state.</term> known by few, we desired to join together a benign spirit—a battle winning against fire [and not overcome by it]. Although we have felt it many times with the senses of the body and seen it with our own eyes, we did not have the knowledge of its extraction through any science or art. Feeling ourselves still supported by a certain lack of refinement <note>original: *rusticitate*; implying a lack of sophisticated understanding or "clumsiness" in the art</note>, we were in no way able to fully comprehend it, until another **spirit of Prophecy** <note>original: *spiritus Prophetiae*</note>, breathing down from the **Father of Lights** <note>a reference to God, derived from James 1:17; the author frames alchemical discovery as divine revelation</note>, descended. He by no means deserts his own, nor fails those who ask of him; in **waking dreams** <note>original: *somniis vigilantibus*; a state of visionary clarity or focused meditation</note>, he poured such clarity into the eyes of our mind that he deigned to reveal that [spirit] freely, both inside and out, with the veil of allegory removed. 

Refreshing us with insatiable goodness, he demonstrated that to fulfill this, we should prepare the body for a single, **secret natural decoction** <term>decoction: <note>original: *decoctionem*</note>; the process of applying a steady, gentle heat to "cook" or mature the alchemical substance.</term>. By this, entirely in **reverse order** <note>original: *ordine retrogrado*; likely referring to a process of deconstruction or "going back" to the primary matter</note> and with a **piercing lance** <note>original: *pungenti lancea*; a metaphor for a sharp, transformative agent or a specific stage of chemical "attack" on the material</note>, its entire nature is immediately turned into pure blackness...

### *On the Augmentative Power and on the Exuberance of Mercury through the Resolution of Individuals.*

#### CHAPTER LXXI.

The **augmentative power** <note>original: *Augmentatiua*; the process of increasing the potency or volume of the Elixir or "Stone."</note> is that which naturally leads either the sulfur or the stone, by means of the quantity of the **medicine** <note>original: *medicinæ*; a common alchemical term for the catalyst or Philosopher's Stone that "heals" base metals into gold.</note>, to that limit which the **individuals** <term>individuals: <note>original: *indiuidua*; in Lullian alchemy, these are the smallest indivisible particles or "atoms" that make up a substance.</note></term> are able to reach. Therefore, he who is ignorant of individual properties will also be ignorant of the proper regimen of the body and of its exuberance. For the augmentative property, with its powers, exists within the individuals of sulfur and of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti viui*; another name for Mercury.</note>: and therefore, since the individuals of sulfur are very small when combined with the individuals...

Wherefore, if you look closely, it is evident that a small quantity of a natural substance loses much of its own potential when it has been removed or separated from a large quantity. Within a larger mass, it could have been bound by a natural bond in a **fellowship of love** <note>original: *amoris consortio*; a common alchemical metaphor for the chemical affinity or "attraction" between substances of the same nature.</note>, preserving its properties; this is true of the large separated from the greater, and the greater from the greatest. 

For we do not see a man or a goat extended by natural power to the size or magnitude of a tower, beyond that common size which the nature of individuals demands. This occurs by reason of the dissimilarity of the parts as they differ from one another. 

Or yet another reason may exist: because a thing might be, or be capable of being, of a greater quantity than its own genus requires. By reason of its species, a living thing is compelled to exist under a limit of certain magnitude and growth; for we see clearly that it follows a form of a determined quantity, as is evident in all things generated by nature. <page-num>153</page-num>
<header>OF RAYMOND LULLY</header>

...to sharpness; and there is a need, lest by the retention of that [Mercury] during the reduction of fixation, you make its entire middle substance fugitive <note>original: *fugitiuam*; refers to the tendency of alchemical substances to evaporate or escape when heated if they are not properly "fixed."</note> during the projection <term>projection: <note>original: *proiectione*; the final stage of the alchemical work where the finished Stone is cast onto base metals to transmute them into gold or silver.</note></term>.

*There are four natural virtues that serve the three mentioned before; namely, the **attractive**, **elementative**, **retentive**, and **expulsive**.*

### CHAPTER LXXII.

The natural virtues which serve and minister to the three aforesaid are four: namely, the **attractive**, through which the substance of the **stone** attracts from **Mercury** <note>original: *de Mercurio*</note> that which it needs in the craft, and this is done through the **elementative** virtue, desiring to fill a vacuum. The **retentive** virtue is that which retains [the matter] by reason of the desiring vacuum. The **elementative** power and the **digestive** power transmute that which is retained from one disposition into another, such as **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum viuum*; literally "living silver," the liquid metal mercury, seen here as a raw material for the work.</note> into the substance of a coagulating **sulfur** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle, while Mercury is the passive, feminine, and fluid principle.</note>: and afterwards it becomes another coagulating quicksilver, and this by reason of its vegetative power <note>original: *vegetatiuitatis*; the life-like ability of the alchemical matter to grow and multiply itself like a plant.</note> or something like it; the virtue of which proceeds from the **hidden fire** <note>original: *igne... occulto*</note> within the fixed substance of the quicksilver. And this is the **fire of hidden nature**, revealed to the sons of truth <note>original: *filiis veritatis*; a traditional term for initiated alchemists.</note>. By which fire they will be able to do so much in...

It is also necessary for you to know, to complete the correction of your error, that the aforesaid virtues differ in two ways regarding the origin they draw from the **elementalized elements** <note>original: *elementis elementatis*; a scholastic term referring to the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) as they exist in physical, compound bodies rather than in their pure, theoretical state.</note>. For some of them are **innate**, and some are **influencing**. Those are **innate virtues** which belong properly to the elements of the **elementalized stone**. Moreover, the innate virtue of each element is so-called because it does not cling to it from the influence of another element, as is evident after their division through the effect in which it is shown that its own virtue does not fail to adhere <note>The text suggests that even when elements are separated, they retain their core, "innate" characteristics.</note>...

...except from the proper and intrinsic principles of its own **generation** <note>original: *generationi*; the process by which a substance is brought into being.</note>. From this, it will be clearly evident to you that each and every elementalized element is governed either by the **innate virtue** <note>original: *virtute innata*</note> of the **stone** <note>original: *lapidis*; the material being transformed in the alchemical Work.</note> alone, or by the innate and influencing virtues together. This is properly demonstrated in the **reductions of conjunctions** <note>original: *reductionibus coniunctionum*; the alchemical stage of breaking down combined substances into their base parts to re-unite them in a more perfect state.</note> of substances that can be mixed and have already been altered. It is sufficient for the practitioner of this **mastery** <term>mastery: <note>original: *magisterij*; the specialized art of alchemy or the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term> to understand this; nor will it be an obstacle to set aside the idea that those elementalized elements are governed only by innate virtues which have descended from **sensible principles** <note>original: *principiis sensibilibus*; the physical, observable origins of matter.</note>, into whose channels they have not yet entered. Instead, they are governed by innate and influencing virtues, which receive emanations from those principles through some sensible channel.

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>CODICILLUS</header>

...for this purpose: so that it might be able to correctly rectify **errors** <note>original: *peccata*; literally "sins," but in an alchemical context, it refers to defects or failures in the chemical process.</note> in the operation, and also to preserve **perfections**. Those instruments, therefore, through which the **innate virtues** <note>original: *innatæ virtutes*; the natural powers inherent to a substance's essence.</note> produce their operations and bring them to completion within **elementalized elements** <note>original: *elementis elementatis*; physical bodies composed of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note>, are **composition** and **complexion** <term>complexion: <note>original: *complexio*; the specific balance of the four primary qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) that determines the nature of a body.</note></term>. 

From this, it follows that every power and virtue which exercises **local motion** <note>original: *localem motum*; physical movement from one place to another.</note> by its own nature makes use of composition immediately. And even if such a virtue for exercising local motion is not inherently present [as a whole], there is nonetheless within it a virtue that exercises a motion that is "immobile" in respect to its place. And although this "immobile" motion is there primarily and principally, it nonetheless pertains to location. 

In a second and subsequent way, motion is present within it through the **attraction of nourishment** <note>original: *attractione alimenti*</note>. For, as has been proven in the **books on animals** <note>likely referring to Aristotle’s *De Animalibus*, which was a standard authority on biology and physiology.</note>, nourishment moves toward the parts of that which is being nourished, just as an object moves toward its natural place. 

But this attraction of food—in regard to the place toward which the nourishment is drawn—is the **power of earth** <note>original: *vis terræ*</note>. This earth, though its hollow spaces are full of air, acts with abundance to draw moisture to itself; this occurs even though the "form" of the nourishment, through the **digestive heat** bestowed upon it, mixes the food with the earth. Furthermore, the power of the earthy element draws nourishment to itself; just as a place draws an object to it, so does the nourishment of that which...

...is nourished, received within its own **porosity** <note>original: *spongiositatem*; refers to the physical texture of a substance that allows for absorption or "sponginess."</note>. From this, it necessarily follows that such a power makes use of **composition** <note>original: *compositione*; the physical arrangement or structural assembly of parts.</note> secondarily or subsequently. However, the other powers use **complexion** <term>complexion: <note>original: *complexio*; the specific internal balance of the four primary qualities—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—that defines a substance's nature.</note></term> immediately; this includes the aforementioned power when it does not perform local motion. The artisan experiences this most clearly through the four natural **servants** <note>original: *naturalibus ministrantibus*; referring to the four biological/chemical functions of attraction, retention, digestion, and expulsion.</note>. 

The natural composition of the **earth of the stone** <note>original: *terræ lapidis*; the solid, material foundation of the alchemical matter.</note>, because it partakes of **heat** <note>original: *caliditatis*</note>, is able to attract as much as it needs. Because it partakes of **dryness** <note>original: *siccitatis*</note>, it can retain as much as is sufficient. Because of **moisture** <note>original: *humiditatis*</note>, it can expel; and because of both heat and moisture, it can digest. 

And thus it happens that a single quality—such as that found in **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphure*; in the Lullian tradition, sulfur is the active, masculine principle and the source of "heat."</note>, which is composed of the diverse qualities of the four elements—becomes a single principle for various operations: specifically in the composition of the **Elixir** <note>original: *Elixiris*; the perfected medicinal or transmutative substance.</note>. Natural heat and flowing spirits serve this quality or principle as instruments. Nevertheless, whether in the **formed stone** <note>original: *lapide formato*; the completed Alchemical Stone.</note>, the composed Elixir, or even in the **exhausted earth** <note>original: *terra depopulata*; literally "depopulated" or "plundered" earth, referring to the inert material residue left after the vital principles have been extracted.</note>, there exists only one actual quality: the **complexion** through which all its natural powers bring their operations to completion. One reason for this is because they are the active principle of natural operations in any body, serving as the instruments of its inner virtue. Another reason is because, through their own nature, they bring about changes or alterations in any body. For dryness and moisture alter a body according to nature by making an impression upon it just as well as the other two qualities <note>heat and cold</note>, although they do not do so quite as quickly or as strongly.

Once you have cleansed the aforementioned **reductions** <term>Reductions: <note>original: *reductiones*; the process of returning a substance to its primary or liquid state.</note></term> from the corrupting, non-fixed **sulfurousness** <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the principle of combustion and "staining." However, when you see the purest nature of the earth raised upward, clinging to the segments of the **aludel** <term>Aludel: <note>A series of pear-shaped pots or condensers used in the sublimation process to collect the purified vapor as it cools and solidifies.</note></term> like a dead thing, you must repeat the sublimation upon it. 

Do this without the dregs that remain at the bottom, because the fixed <note>stable or non-volatile</note> part of the matter would stick to those dregs, and then no one, through any cleverness or skill, would be able to separate them. 

That material which has been sublimated onto the surface of the aludel—separated from the dregs as well as from its own middle substance—in the form of a most subtle powder, brings about a burning along with corruption. This happens even though, for the most part, during the piercing of the vapor at the time of conception or reduction (specifically in **calcinations** <term>Calcination: <note>original: *calcinationibus*; the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to a powder or "calx."</note></term>), that which is more subtle and intense—the smoky or "sooty" part—flies higher and away from the pure vapor, and from the substance of the retention of **fixation**...

...[substan]ce <note>The text begins with the suffix of a word from the previous page, likely *substantia* (substance).</note> is separated, since it is not of the substance of **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: <note>original: *argenti vivi*; literally "living silver," the common name for mercury.</note></term>, nor of its perfection. Rather, it is the **blessed stone** <note>original: *lapis benedictus*</note> and a spirit participating in two extremities. 

Nevertheless, although the stone is made from its middle substance, it is simply called quicksilver, because its sulfur illuminates it and preserves it from burning; this is its own particular sign, acting as the cause of its perfection.

### CODICILLVS

...without mixing, because you should not take it except recently after birth <note>original: *post partum*; here referring to the emergence of the substance from the chemical process.</note> with its own blood <note>original: *cum sanguine suo*; in alchemy, "blood" refers to the essential red tincture or life-force of the matter.</note>, so that the venerable nature—which was weakened, exhausted, and pulled away or distanced from its proper balance by the violence of its instability—may be strengthened with its own **white ferments** <note>original: *fermentis albis*; catalytic substances used to "leaven" the alchemical matter and give it the quality of the white stage.</note>, until it reaches a better and more stable state.

The sign of this will be when it flows over the slightest fire—specifically before the flight <note>original: *fugam*; the point at which the substance would normally evaporate or sublimate.</note> of the **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: <note>original: *argenti viui*; another name for Mercury, often considered the "mother" or the fluid base of all metals.</note></term>. Furthermore, once it has been increased, it will remain at the end: stable and penetrating, deepening, dyeing, and persevering.

However, when you wish to obtain the **Red Elixir** <term>Red Elixir: <note>original: *Elixir rubeum*; the final, most potent form of the Philosopher's Stone, capable of transmuting metals into gold.</note></term>—by which I mean the visible natural fire in its color, acting as **red sulfur** <term>Red sulfur: <note>original: *sulphure rubeo*; sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle in alchemy.</note></term>—you must do this through the third conjunction, the third pregnancy, and the third birth.

Therefore, make the male ascend above the white woman <note>A metaphor for the union of the active "red" principle with the passive "white" or lunar principle.</note>, so that he may beget a son like himself. For a father naturally desires to multiply his own kind in a son, so that he may preserve in his offspring the nature which he cannot keep forever in himself. And for this reason, for that generation to occur, it is necessary that he pours out some of his own substance. The father of this [offspring] is the **Sun** <note>original: *Sol*; representing Gold or the masculine soul.</note>, and the **Moon** <note>original: *Luna*; representing Silver or the feminine spirit.</note> is the mother, both having been reduced to their first simple matter.

<summary>This page describes the final stabilization of the white stage and the transition to the "Red Elixir" through a metaphorical "third birth." It explains that the Sun (Gold/Male) and Moon (Silver/Female) must be joined so the father can pass his nature to a more permanent "son" or offspring.</summary>

# RAYMOND LULL

<page-num>165</page-num>

->L iij<-

**Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: <note>original: *Argentum vivum*; Mercury, considered here as the foundational substance into which other materials are reduced.</note></term> is also that into whose nature they have been reduced, and it is the medium that produces the nature of their conjunction. This is because when quicksilver is joined with quicksilver, they are never again separated, just as water mixed with water cannot be divided.

Therefore, join the **masculine sperm**—that is, the hidden **red sulfur** <term>Red sulfur: <note>original: *sulphur rubeum*; the active, solar, masculine principle in alchemy, associated here with gold.</note></term> dissolved in water—with the **feminine sperm** collected earlier in the form of **white sulfur**. This is achieved through the methods of grinding, soaking <term>Imbibition: <note>original: *imbibitionis*; the process of a solid substance absorbing a liquid.</note></term>, and slow cooking <term>Decoction: <note>original: *decoctionis*; the application of heat to extract or refine substances.</note></term>, until it dissolves into a "red water" and the whole becomes a single, perfectly unified mixture.

Indeed, when it has been dissolved and made into one thing, let it be congealed <note>original: *congeletur*; solidified from a liquid state.</note> by means of reduction. Once it is coagulated, dissolve it into water again, and once more congeal it into a **stone** <note>original: *lapidem*; referring to the Philosopher's Stone in its developmental phase.</note> through the method shown above. A third time, dissolve it in water and cook it until the entire substance becomes red.

My son, he who does not join substances by their smallest parts <note>original: *per minima*; a fundamental alchemical concept of mixing substances at the most minute, essential level rather than just a surface level.</note> does not truly refine them. Nor can he who dries the matter before soaking it join it by its smallest parts. Therefore, soak the whole and dry it, until it becomes an extremely fine powder—that is, dissolved not into the water of a cloud <note>meaning common rainwater or simple moisture.</note>, but into a "mercurial water of blood" <note>original: *aquam sanguinis & mercurialem*; a potent, life-giving alchemical solvent or "tincture."</note>. Then, apply a very strong fire to it, so that...

<summary>This page describes the "alchemical marriage" or conjunction of the masculine (red sulfur) and feminine (white sulfur) principles, emphasizing the need for repeated cycles of dissolution and coagulation to achieve a perfect, inseparable mixture.</summary>

# CODICILLVS

<page-num>166</page-num>

Let the untinged white substance be separated, and let the remainder be gilded, tinged, digested, cooked, and **turned red** <note>original: *rubificetur*; the final coloring stage of the alchemical process, indicating the achievement of the "Red Work" or rubedo.</note>. That which rises upward in the form of a very fine powder is the **whitest sublimated sulfur**. 

However, that which remains at the bottom below is the **fixed sulfur**, turned red by the fire of the stone, which previously could not be seen. It is with this that craftsmen make the **elixir** <term>Elixir: <note>original: *elixir*; a medicinal or transmutational substance capable of perfecting imperfect metals.</note></term> for gold. For that elixir converts **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: <note>original: *argentum vivum*; Mercury.</note></term> through artifice into the most true **gold**. At this step, we reveal that the philosophers spoke the truth, which the ignorant and the unlearned consider impossible. 

And we were similarly of the opinion not to believe this at all for a very long time, until in the end we saw and proved it with our own hands; namely, that there is **one stone**, one medicine, one nature, one disposition, one work, and one vessel for making both the white and the red sulfur, together and at once. 

Although all investigators of this art, seeing the aforementioned **whiteness** <note>original: *albedinem*; the silver-making stage of the process.</note> appearing in the vessel, do not believe that **redness** <note>original: *rubedinem*; the gold-making stage.</note> is hidden within it. For you must not, my son, if you wish to convert the whole of the white sulfur into red, extract the aforementioned whiteness by **sublimation** <term>Sublimation: <note>original: *sublimationem*; the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note></term> ...

<summary>This page explains the separation of volatile white sulfur from fixed red sulfur within the same vessel, asserting that the "Red Elixir" for making gold is hidden within the white stage. The author claims to have personally verified these claims through experimental practice, refuting the doubts of the unlearned.</summary>
